## Page 7


Preface 2026
Since the publication of the first edition, numerous errors in the text have been pointed out to
me, ranging from simple typographical errors, embarrassing spelling mistakes, inappropriate
and unwelcome copy-pastes, and even some mathematical issues.
I would like to warmly thank Ammar Yasir Kiliç, Eyal Kaplan, Yuta Nakayama, and Professor
Naoki Imai who pointed out many of these errors to me. I have made available on my webpage
https://perso.pages.math.cnrs.fr/users/david.renard/recherche.html
a continuously corrected version, as well as an erratum detailing the changes made.
Given the progress made by AI in mathematical writing, it seemed appropriate to use it to
carry out a complete proofreading of the text and detect remaining errors (specifically Google
AI Studio Gemini 3.1 pro-preview). The result is overwhelming; the list of typos found is
impressively long. It seems justified to make this new version accessible to as many people as
possible, by having it translated into English at the same time. The writing and any remaining
errors are my own; the only proof writing entrusted to the AI is that of the injectivity of the
map defined in (VI.5.1.3), which seems entirely convincing to me, unlike the proof I had
written trying to follow that of [20], which was unfortunately completely wrong.
It does not seem useful to me to publish this new version as a book with an official publisher.
The proofreading work done at the time by the reviewers was excellent, and the value added
by the publisher was absolutely zero. In fact, a (small and unimportant) part of the numerous
typos is due to the processing of my LaTeX file by the latter, and running it through a spell
checker, which my LaTeX editor at the time did not offer, could have proved useful. It is highly
likely that a new publisher would not do any better.
I was deeply impressed by the efficiency of the help provided by the AI. I am curious to
see the evolution of mathematical text production in the near future.

## Page 9


Preface to the 2010 Edition
The goal of this book is to present, in a more or less complete form, the foundations of the
representation theory of 𝑝-adic reductive groups. This subject blends a geometric perspective,
which can only be developed through the careful study of explicit examples, and the use of
very general results and principles, falling under what is often called "abstract nonsense", i.e.,
category theory 1. One of the main promoters of the use of these techniques is J. Bernstein,
to whom many fundamental results are due, in particular those around what is called the
Bernstein center.
The initial idea was to present it as sketched in the more or less official notes of J. Bernstein
[2], [3], but which has not, to our knowledge, been completely presented in a publishable
form. There are older texts where one can find the foundations of the theory and the proofs
of its main results, due to Bernstein, Casselman, Harish-Chandra, Howe, Jacquet, Zelevinskii
([4], [5], [20], [41]). Then there is the very clear and complete write-up by P. Deligne [24]
of Bernstein’s ideas on the "center". However, the texts [2] and [3], subsequent to Deligne’s,
propose a significantly different approach to these results, which seems to us even more
elegant and of greater scope. On the other hand, specialists in the field have sometimes made
their notes on the subject available on the web, giving or completing the proofs of [2], [3],
for example [23], [38]. But none of these texts covers the entirety of Bernstein’s theory. The
need for a book therefore became apparent. Conversations with colleagues confirmed this
impression, which motivated me to undertake this task. My background being in real rather
than 𝑝-adic groups, I was not the best placed for this work, which is undoubtedly reflected in
the imperfections of this text. But as many know, the best way to learn a subject is to teach it
to students, or to write a book on it. I nevertheless hope that others, novices or specialists,
will benefit from it.
Let us now give an overview of the book’s content, as well as indications on how to read it.
Each chapter is preceded by an introduction describing its content and is followed by notes
where we have tried to attribute the results and indicated the sources used for writing the
proofs. We will therefore be brief in this general introduction.
The theory of smooth representations of 𝑝-adic reductive groups is presented in Chapters
VI and VII, which constitute the core of the book. The main tools of this study are the functors
of parabolic induction and restriction, the notion of supercuspidal representation, and their
finiteness properties arising from the fine structure of 𝑝-adic reductive groups. The category
of smooth representations of a 𝑝-adic reductive group is studied in Chapter VI, where, among
other results, the Bernstein decomposition theorem and the determination of the center of this
category are established. Chapter VII deals with square-integrable representations, tempered
representations, intertwining operators, and culminates with the Langlands classification
theorem.
The preceding chapters place this study in a more general framework: a 𝑝-adic reductive
group is in particular a totally disconnected topological group. The representation theory of
this class of groups is developed in Chapters III and IV. Chapter II presents general results
1The most important concept in this regard is that of adjunction, which is embodied in representation theory
under the name of "Frobenius reciprocity".
3


## Page 10


4
Contents
on the topology of totally disconnected spaces (functions, distributions, sheaves) and totally
disconnected groups (Haar measure, convolution, Hecke algebra). The language of category
theory is used extensively: let 𝐺be a totally disconnected topological group. The main object
of study in Chapter III is the category M(𝐺) of smooth (i.e., continuous) representations of 𝐺
in C-vector spaces. We show that this category is equivalent to that of non-degenerate modules
over the Hecke algebra of 𝐺, which is an algebra with idempotents. The notions of an algebra
with idempotents and of non-degenerate modules for such an algebra are the subject of Chapter
I of the book. Many results on the category M(𝐺) then follow immediately from those obtained
in the purely algebraic framework of Chapter I. In particular, the forgetful and base change
functors from module theory yield functors between categories of smooth representations of
totally disconnected groups. Chapter IV studies specific classes of representations: compact,
unitary, and square-integrable modulo the center representations. Compact representations
behave like representations of a compact group, and their properties are absolutely essential
for the rest of the theory.
To go further, one must restrict the class of groups studied to groups possessing more
structure. 𝑝-adic reductive groups have a very rich structure, which comes on the one hand
from the general theory of reductive algebraic groups, and on the other hand from Bruhat-Tits
theory. These structural results are simply recalled in Chapter V. However, we have tried to
present them carefully, introducing the necessary notation, ordering them, and giving precise
references for the unproven results.
This book does not necessarily have to be read linearly. The reader already having some
knowledge of the subject can start reading directly at Chapter VI and refer to the preceding
chapters as needed.
The first four chapters require very few mathematical prerequisites, but some familiarity
with the language of category theory is necessary. The rudiments of this theory are given in
Appendix A. Chapters VI and VII are only accessible to readers with a good knowledge of
reductive groups, and Chapter V can at most serve as a guide for the student who will have to
refer to the given bibliography. In particular, the study of examples is indispensable at this
stage, and the reader will not find any in this book.
Acknowledgments
I would like to warmly thank J. Bernstein for giving his consent to the writing of this book
presenting many (but not all) of his ideas on the representation theory of 𝑝-adic reductive
groups. I would also like to thank the specialists in the subject who kindly answered my
questions during my learning process: A. Badulescu, G. Chenevier, G. Muic, P. Garrett, and
more particularly S. DeBacker, A. Roche, and J-F. Dat who also agreed to let me reuse,
practically word for word, certain proofs from their notes or articles. Y. Laszlo helped me,
with patience, to understand some notions of algebraic geometry, which are necessary for
reading (and therefore writing) this book. An anonymous referee noticed numerous errors,
sometimes embarrassing, and suggested some simplifications of proofs.
Of course, the remaining errors and imperfections are my sole responsibility. Finally, S.
Aicardi’s absolute knowledge of the arcana of computer science and the mysteries of LaTeX
was invaluable.


## Page 11


CHAPTER I
Algebras with Idempotents
The representation theory of a finite group 𝐺(say, with values in complex vector spaces)
reduces to the study of the category M(C[𝐺]) of unital modules over the group algebra C[𝐺].
Multiple constructions and results then follow. For more general groups, an important idea
is to define a "good" category of representations to study, i.e., rich enough to contain the
representations we are likely to be interested in, and for which we can nevertheless show
that it is equivalent to a category of modules over a certain C-algebra H (𝐺), so that the
constructions made in the case of finite groups generalize. It is possible to define such a
category when 𝐺is the group of rational points of a connected reductive algebraic group
defined over a local field F. In the case where F is a 𝑝-adic field, we will see in the following
chapters the definition of the category of smooth representations of the group 𝐺, and the
equivalence of this category with a category of modules over a certain C-algebra H (𝐺), the
Hecke algebra of 𝐺.
Similarly, if F is Archimedean, the category of Harish-Chandra modules is equivalent to
a certain category of modules over the Hecke algebra of 𝐺(see [35]). In both cases, these
Hecke algebras are not unital. This could a priori ruin our strategy, because the constructions
for finite groups crucially use the fact that one considers the category of unital modules
over the unital algebra C[𝐺]. It turns out that the Hecke algebras in question are equipped
with a structure close to that given by an identity element. We use the terminology "algebra
with idempotents" for this structure, whereas [35] uses that of an algebra with approximate
identity, which perhaps conveys more the idea of a generalization of the unital case. In this
chapter, we study algebras with idempotents, and their categories of non-degenerate modules
(a notion that generalizes that of unital modules over a unital ring), as well as certain functors
between these categories.
Notation and Conventions. We will use the following notation throughout the text:
Z −mod for the category of abelian groups,
𝑘−Vect for the category of vector spaces over a field 𝑘.
If 𝐴and 𝐵are rings:
𝐴−mod for the category of left 𝐴-modules,
mod −𝐴for the category of right 𝐴-modules,
𝐴−mod −𝐵for the category of 𝐴−𝐵-bimodules.
Recall that 𝑀is an 𝐴−𝐵-bimodule if 𝑀is equipped with a left 𝐴-module structure and a
right 𝐵-module structure, and the actions of 𝐴and 𝐵on 𝑀commute.
If 𝑉is a vector space, we denote by 𝑉∗its algebraic dual, and by ⟨. , . ⟩the canonical bilinear
form on 𝑉∗× 𝑉.

Chapter I. Algebras with Idempotents
Rings are not assumed to be unital unless explicitly stated otherwise. If 𝐴is a unital ring ,
we denote by 1𝐴its identity element. If 𝐴is a unital ring, modules over 𝐴are not assumed to
be unital a priori. If they are (i.e., if 1𝐴acts as the identity), we specify this by saying that the
𝐴-module 𝑀is unital.
In general, if 𝑒is an idempotent of 𝐴, i.e., such that 𝑒2 = 𝑒(for example, if 𝐴is unital,
𝑒= 1𝐴), and if 𝑀is an 𝐴-module, then 𝑀, as a Z-module, decomposes as:
𝑀= 𝑒· 𝑀⊕(1 −𝑒) · 𝑀,
where 𝑒· 𝑀= {𝑒· 𝑚| 𝑚∈𝑀} and (1 −𝑒) · 𝑀= {𝑚−𝑒· 𝑚| 𝑚∈𝑀}. The idempotent 𝑒acts as
the identity on 𝑒· 𝑀and annihilates (1 −𝑒) · 𝑀. The ring 𝑒𝐴𝑒is unital (with identity 𝑒), and
𝑒· 𝑀is a unital 𝑒𝐴𝑒-module.
If 𝐴is a unital ring, we denote by M(𝐴) the full subcategory of 𝐴−mod whose objects
are the unital 𝐴-modules.
Remark. The notation (1−𝑒) · 𝑀is merely a notational convenience, and one should be careful
that the 1 here does not denote the identity element of the ring 𝐴.

I.1.2
Rings with Idempotents: Definitions
Definition. Let 𝐴be a ring (not a priori unital). We say that 𝐴is a ring with idempotents
if for any finite set of elements {𝑎𝑖} of 𝐴, there exists an idempotent 𝑒in 𝐴(𝑒2 = 𝑒) such
that 𝑎𝑖= 𝑒𝑎𝑖𝑒for all 𝑖.

1. A unital ring is a ring with idempotents, and its unital modules are exactly the non-

idempotents 𝐴. It is a full subcategory of the category 𝐴−mod of all modules over 𝐴. One
easily verifies that this subcategory is stable under passing to submodules and quotients: it is
therefore an abelian category (cf. A.VI.2). In the case where 𝐴is a unital ring, M(𝐴) is then
the category of unital 𝐴-modules, which is consistent with the notation introduced previously.
We will also sometimes need to consider right modules. We denote by M(𝐴)𝑟the category of
non-degenerate right 𝐴-modules.
Proposition. The functor 𝐴−mod →M(𝐴), 𝑀↦→𝑀𝐴is exact.
Proof. First, note that if 𝑓: 𝑀→𝑁is a morphism in 𝐴−mod, it sends 𝑀𝐴into 𝑁𝐴. This
defines the functor on morphisms by restriction to the non-degenerate part. If

We refer the reader to [10] or [26] for the generalities on topological spaces used in this
section, such as the notions of a family of pseudometrics, uniform structure, completion, etc.
Let 𝐴be a ring with idempotents and let 𝑒∈ℐ= Idem(𝐴). As a Z-module, we have a

There exists a natural topology on 𝐴: a neighborhood basis of 0 is given by the subsets

I.1.6
Other Descriptions of 𝐴
Since 𝐴is naturally, by left multiplication, a non-degenerate left 𝐴-module, it is also a unital
left 𝐴-module, and any element ¯𝑎∈𝐴defines an endomorphism of abelian groups of 𝐴which
commutes with right multiplications. One can therefore identify (cf. remark I.1.4) 𝐴with the
subgroup End𝐴◦(𝐴) of EndZ(𝐴).
Let us equip EndZ(𝐴) with the topology of pointwise convergence: it is a topological
abelian group (for addition) where a neighborhood basis of 0 is given by the family of open
sets
O(𝑈1, . . . , 𝑈𝑛, 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑛) := {𝛽∈EndZ(𝐴) | 𝛽(𝑎𝑖) ∈𝑈𝑖}
where the 𝑎𝑖are elements of 𝐴, and the 𝑈𝑖are open neighborhoods of 0 in 𝐴. One can take
the 𝑈𝑖of the form 𝑈𝑖= 𝐴(1 −𝑒𝑖) with 𝑒𝑖∈ℐ. The induced topology on 𝐴then coincides with
the one defined previously. Indeed
O(𝑈1, . . . , 𝑈𝑛, 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑛) ∩𝐴= {¯𝑎∈𝐴| ¯𝑎𝑎𝑖∈𝑈𝑖},
so if the 𝑈𝑖are of the form 𝑈𝑖= 𝐴(1 −𝑒𝑖) with 𝑒𝑖∈ℐ, and if we take 𝑓∈ℐsuch that
𝑓𝑎𝑖= 𝑎𝑖for all 𝑖, then for all ¯𝑎∈𝐴(1 −𝑓), we have ¯𝑎𝑎𝑖= ¯𝑎𝑓𝑎𝑖= 0, which shows that
𝐴(1 −𝑓) ⊂O(𝑈1, . . . , 𝑈𝑛, 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑛) ∩𝐴. Conversely, 𝐴(1 −𝑓) = O(𝐴(1 −𝑓), 𝑓) ∩𝐴.
It is clear that 𝐴is contained in the closure, for this topology of pointwise convergence,
of the set of left multiplications by elements of 𝐴. Conversely, this closure is of course itself
contained in the commutant of right multiplications, i.e., in End𝐴◦(𝐴) = 𝐴.
We have thus obtained the following lemma.
Lemma. The ring 𝐴identifies with the closure in EndZ(𝐴), for the topology of pointwise
convergence, of the set of left multiplications by elements of 𝐴.
One can view 𝐴in yet another way. Let

be the forgetful functor.

the forgetful functor to itself the element 𝜃𝐴∈EndZ(𝐴).

ring structure. Let 𝜃be a natural transformation from 𝜛to itself. Let 𝑓in Hom𝐴(𝐴, 𝐴) be a
morphism given by a right multiplication in 𝐴. From the definition of a natural transformation,

such that 𝑎· 𝑚≠0.

reductive group, Bernstein’s description of the center is then essentially the determination of

Let 𝐴and 𝐵be unital C-algebras, and 𝜙: 𝐴→𝐵a morphism of unital algebras. The "forgetful

∗𝜙: 𝑀↦→𝐵⊗𝐴𝑀
and the right adjoint 𝜙∗by the Hom functor
𝜙∗: 𝑀↦→Hom𝐴(𝐵, 𝑀).
Note also that we adopt the conventions of algebraic geometry: 𝜙↦→𝜙∗is contravariant,
but despite this we put the star as a subscript rather than a superscript. This is because from
a geometric point of view we consider 𝜙as a morphism between the varieties Spec(𝐵) and
Spec(𝐴), which restores covariance.
On the other hand, in the construction of ∗𝜙(resp. 𝜙∗), we only use the right 𝐴-module

𝐴-module structure on 𝐵induced by 𝜙and the natural right module structure of 𝐵on itself)
and the fact that the left and right actions commute. It is therefore interesting to describe the
forgetful functor 𝜙∗in terms of these a priori weaker structures. Now, for any unital 𝐵-module

𝑁≃Hom𝐵(𝐵, 𝑁) ≃𝐵⊗𝐵𝑁,

of 𝐵and the fact that the left and right actions commute. The adjunctions between 𝜙∗and
∗𝜙, 𝜙∗are then consequences of the second identity of Theorem I.2.2.

In certain cases, the functor 𝑗𝑒is in fact an equivalence of categories between M(𝐴, 𝑒) and
M(𝑒𝐴𝑒). The following result gives a criterion for this.
Theorem. The functor 𝑗𝑒is an equivalence of categories between M(𝐴, 𝑒) and M(𝑒𝐴𝑒) if
and only if the subcategory M(𝐴, 𝑒) of M(𝐴) is stable under passing to subquotients (in
particular, it is then an abelian category). The inverse functor is given by the functor 𝑖= 𝑃𝐴
𝑒𝐴𝑒.

if 𝑊is an 𝐴-submodule of 𝑉, then 𝑊and 𝑉/𝑊are still in M(𝐴, 𝑒). It is clear that if 𝐴𝑒· 𝑉= 𝑉,
then 𝐴𝑒· (𝑉/𝑊) = 𝑉/𝑊. It remains to show that 𝑊∈M(𝐴, 𝑒). On the other hand, by replacing
𝑉with 𝑉/𝐴𝑒· 𝑊, we reduce to the case where 𝐴𝑒· 𝑊= 0. We then need to show that 𝑊= 0.
Consider the canonical projection 𝑝: 𝑉→𝑉/𝑊, and its image under the functor 𝑗𝑒,
𝑗𝑒(𝑝) : 𝑒· 𝑉→𝑒· (𝑉/𝑊).
Since 𝑗𝑒is exact, 𝑗𝑒(𝑝) is surjective, but we also see that it is injective. Indeed, if 𝑒· 𝑣= 𝑤∈𝑊,
we have 𝑒· 𝑣= 𝑤= 𝑒· 𝑤= 0 since 𝑒·𝑊= 0. If we assume that 𝑗𝑒is an equivalence of categories,
the projection 𝑝must be an isomorphism, and then 𝑊= 0.

the submodule 𝐹(𝑒𝐴, 𝑉) is trivial. The proof of the previous proposition then suggests that
the inverse of 𝑗𝑒is given by the induction functor 𝑖. We have already shown in (I.3.2.1) that

I.5.3
Free Modules
Let 𝐴be a C-algebra with idempotents. We say that 𝑀∈M(𝐴) is a free module if it is
isomorphic to a direct sum of modules isomorphic to 𝐴.
Every module 𝑀is a quotient of a free module. The proof is similar to that of the fact
that M(𝐴) has enough projectives. Indeed, let 𝑀∈M(𝐴), and let 𝑚∈𝑀, fixed by a certain
idempotent 𝑒of 𝐴. Then 𝑚is in the image of the morphism 𝜙: 𝐴→𝑀, defined by 𝜙(𝑎) = 𝑎· 𝑚.
We thus construct a surjective morphism from É
𝑚∈𝑀𝐴to 𝑀.
Unlike the case of unital rings, 𝐴is not necessarily projective in M(𝐴), and therefore a
direct factor of a free module is not necessarily projective. On the other hand, every projective
module is a direct factor of a free module, the proof remaining the same as in the case of
unital rings (cf. [36]).

Let 𝐴be a C-algebra with idempotents. Since the category M(𝐴) is a full subcategory of

the reader to Appendix A.VI for generalities on abelian categories and the terminology used.

and AB6 (cf. A.VI). Inductive limits in M(𝐴) are exact.

## Page 35


CHAPTER II
Totally disconnected spaces and
groups
In this chapter, we develop the study of locally compact totally disconnected (t.d. for short)
topological spaces (and more particularly groups), i.e., such that each point of the space admits
a neighborhood basis of compact open sets. Indeed, 𝑝-adic reductive groups are equipped with
such a topology, coming from that of the fields over which they are defined. The appropriate
notion of a "smooth" function on such spaces is that of a locally constant function. A smooth
function with compact support is then a linear combination of characteristic functions of
disjoint compact open sets. This fact gives functional analysis on these spaces a purely
algebraic character, which makes it much simpler than in the case of differentiable manifolds,
for example. The same is true for the theory of sheaves (of abelian groups) on such a space.
We also study continuous actions of t.d. topological groups on t.d. topological spaces. Here
again, the theory is free from the pathologies encountered in the general case, or even in
the differential framework. A fundamental tool in the study of locally compact topological
groups is the Haar measure. A Haar measure is a measure invariant under the action by
left translation of the group on itself. The proof of the existence and uniqueness up to a
scalar factor of such a measure on locally compact totally disconnected topological groups is
quite elementary. The action by right translation of the group on the Haar measure defines a
"modular" function (or character) on the group. The calculation of this function can be carried
out in terms of indices of compact open subgroups.
Another fundamental notion for the rest of the theory is that of the convolution of distri-
butions. It is this convolution that will allow us to define the algebras playing a role in the
representation theory of t.d. topological groups. The space of compactly supported distribu-
tions on such a group 𝐺becomes an algebra once equipped with the convolution product,
whose identity element is the Dirac distribution at the identity element of the group. The
Haar measures on the compact open subgroups of 𝐺provide a directed system of idempotents
for this algebra. The Hecke algebra of 𝐺is the subalgebra consisting of compactly supported
distributions fixed by left and right convolution by one of these idempotents. We obtain an
algebra with idempotents (without identity), denoted H (𝐺). The theory of Chapter I applies
to this algebra. We explicitly describe the completion and the center of the category of non-
degenerate H (𝐺)-modules in terms of so-called essentially compactly supported distributions.
The importance of these results comes from the fact that the category of non-degenerate
H (𝐺)-modules is equivalent to the category of smooth representations of 𝐺, which will be the
object of study in the next chapter.
29

Chapter II. T.d. spaces and groups
II.1
Topology of t.d. spaces
In this section, we give some general results on the topology of locally compact totally
disconnected spaces.
If 𝑋is a set and 𝑌a subset of 𝑋, we denote by 𝜒𝑌the characteristic function of 𝑌in 𝑋. If 𝑋
is a topological space and 𝑌is a subset of 𝑋, we denote by 𝑌the closure of 𝑌in 𝑋.
II.1.1
T.d. spaces
A locally compact totally disconnected topological space (t.d. space) is a Hausdorff topological
space such that each point admits a neighborhood basis of compact open sets.
A locally compact totally disconnected topological group is a topological group whose
underlying topological space is t.d. To do this, it suffices that the identity element 𝑒admits a
neighborhood basis consisting of compact open subgroups. This condition is in fact necessary,
(Van Dantzig’s theorem). For convenience, one can take this a priori stronger condition as the
definition of t.d. topological groups.
Lemma. (𝑖) Let 𝑋be a t.d. space and 𝑌a locally closed subset of 𝑋. Then 𝑌is a t.d. space
for the induced topology.
(𝑖𝑖) If 𝐾is a compact subset of 𝑋and Ð
𝛼𝑈𝛼is an open cover of 𝐾, then there exists a
finite subordinate cover of 𝐾by disjoint compact open sets 𝑉𝑖(i.e., for all 𝑖, there exists 𝛼
such that 𝑉𝑖⊂𝑈𝛼).
Proof. Recall that 𝑌⊂𝑋is locally closed if for every point 𝑦∈𝑌, there exists a neighborhood
𝑈of 𝑦in 𝑋such that 𝑈∩𝑌is closed in 𝑈. Here, we can assume 𝑈is a compact open set, and (𝑖)
then follows quite easily from the definitions. Let us move on to (𝑖𝑖). For each point 𝑥in some
𝑈𝛼, let us choose a compact open neighborhood 𝑉𝑥,𝛼⊂𝑈𝛼. We then have 𝐾⊂Ð
𝛼,𝑥𝑉𝑥,𝛼, and by
the compactness of 𝐾, we can extract a finite subcover 𝐾⊂Ð
𝑖𝑉𝑥𝑖,𝛼𝑖. Since the 𝑉𝑥𝑖,𝛼𝑖are both
open and closed, the same is true for their pairwise intersections and symmetric differences.
From this, we can easily construct a finite cover of 𝐾satisfying the desired properties in the
statement.
□
Proposition. A t.d. space satisfies the Baire category theorem, i.e., a countable intersection
of dense open sets is dense.
Proof. This is true for any locally compact space (cf. [26], Theorem XI. 10.1).
□
II.1.2
Functions and distributions
Let 𝑋be a t.d. space. We consider the following functional spaces on 𝑋:
𝒞∞(𝑋), the space of locally constant complex-valued functions,
𝒟(𝑋), the subspace of 𝒞∞(𝑋) of compactly supported functions,
𝒟′(𝑋), the (algebraic) dual of 𝒟(𝑋), the space of distributions (note that these spaces are not
equipped with topologies).
Recall that if 𝑓is a complex-valued function on a t.d. space 𝑋, its support, denoted Supp 𝑓,
is defined as the complement of the union of the open sets 𝑈of 𝑋where the restriction of 𝑓is
zero.

II.2
Sheaves on a t.d. topological space
II.2.1
Generalities on sheaf theory
Let 𝑋be a topological space and let T be the topology of 𝑋, i.e., the set of open subsets of 𝑋.
A basis for the topology of 𝑋is a subset T0 ⊂T such that for any 𝑥∈𝑋and for any open set 𝑈
containing 𝑥, there exists an open set 𝑈0 ∈T0 containing 𝑥such that
𝑈0 ⊂𝑈.
Of course, a basis determines the topology.
We will introduce the notions of presheaf and sheaf (of abelian groups) on 𝑋, with basis T0.
This slightly generalizes the definitions given in most treatises on sheaf theory (for example
[29]), which only consider the case T0 = T. This greater generality is in fact only apparent,
since we will see that any sheaf with basis T0 naturally extends to a sheaf with basis T (this
is not true for presheaves), but this flexibility in the definitions is convenient when 𝑋is a
t.d. space, for which the choice T0 = T𝑐, the set of compact open subsets of 𝑋, is particularly
well-suited.
Definition. A presheaf F (of abelian groups) on 𝑋, with basis T0, consists, for each open
set 𝑈in T0, of an abelian group F (𝑈) (if ∅∈T0, then we require that F (∅) = {0}), and
for any pair 𝑈⊂𝑉of open sets in T0, of a restriction morphism
𝜌𝑉,𝑈: F (𝑉) →F (𝑈)
satisfying 𝜌𝑈,𝑈= IdF(𝑈) and when 𝑈⊂𝑉⊂𝑊are elements of T0, 𝜌𝑉,𝑈◦𝜌𝑊,𝑉= 𝜌𝑊,𝑈.
When we assume that the F (𝑈) are equipped with additional structures (rings, vector
spaces over a field 𝑘, etc.), and that the restriction morphisms preserve these structures,
we speak of presheaves of rings, of 𝑘-vector spaces, etc.
An equivalent and slightly more sophisticated way to define presheaves is the following:
let us again denote by T0 the category whose objects are the elements of T0, and whose
morphisms are given by:
HomT0 (𝑈, 𝑉) = ∅

Remark. The sheaf F + and the morphism 𝜄F are solutions to the universal problem (on the
right) posed by F and the forgetful functor from sheaves to presheaves (see A.III.1). There is
therefore uniqueness of (F +, 𝜄F) up to a unique isomorphism. In particular, if F is a sheaf, F
is naturally isomorphic to F + (the isomorphism is 𝜄F). A sheaf is therefore identified with
the sheaf of sections of its étale space bF . When F is a sheaf, we will no longer distinguish
between F and F + in what follows.
Let us end this section by noting that the category of sheaves on 𝑋with basis T0 is equivalent
to the category of sheaves with basis T. One obviously passes from a sheaf with basis T to a
sheaf with basis T0 by restriction. In the other direction, if F is a sheaf with basis T0 and if 𝑈
is any open set of 𝑋, we define F +(𝑈) as the set of sections

category of sheaves on 𝑋with basis T and finishes establishing the equivalence of categories.
II.2.2
Support of a section
We retain the notation of the previous section, in particular F is a sheaf on 𝑋with basis T0.
Definition. Suppose that 𝑠: 𝑈→bF is a section. Its support, denoted Supp(𝑠), is the set
of 𝑥∈𝑈such that 𝑠(𝑥) ≠0.
Proposition. The support of a section 𝑠: 𝑈→bF of the sheaf F is closed in 𝑈.
Proof. Suppose that 𝑥∉Supp(𝑠). By definition of a section, 𝑥has an open neighborhood
𝑊∈T0 such that there exists an element 𝑓∈F (𝑊) with 𝑠(𝑦) = 𝜌𝑊,𝑦( 𝑓) for all 𝑦∈𝑊. Since by
hypothesis 𝜌𝑊,𝑥( 𝑓) = 𝑠(𝑥) = 0, by definition of the inductive limit, there exists a neighborhood
𝑉∈T0 of 𝑥contained in 𝑊such that 𝜌𝑊,𝑉( 𝑓) = 0. Then for all 𝑦∈𝑉,
𝑠(𝑦) = 𝜌𝑉,𝑦◦𝜌𝑊,𝑉( 𝑓) = 0.
This shows that 𝑉does not intersect the support of 𝑠. The complement of this support is
therefore open.
□
By the identification of F and F + we can speak of the support of an element of F (𝑈). If 𝑈
is any open set, we denote by F𝑐(𝑈) the set of elements of F (𝑈) with compact support.

Let R be a sheaf of rings on 𝑋, with basis T0 (we say that (𝑋, R) is a ringed space). A sheaf of
R-modules on 𝑋is a sheaf M on 𝑋such that for any open set 𝑈∈T0, M(𝑈) is a (left) module

morphism by which any R(𝑈)-module becomes an R(𝑉)-module. We then require that the
restriction morphisms 𝜌𝑉,𝑈: M(𝑉) →M(𝑈) be morphisms of R(𝑉)-modules.
II.2.4
Characterization of sheaves on a t.d. space
Let 𝑋be a t.d. space. The set T𝑐of compact open subsets of 𝑋is a basis for the topology
of 𝑋. We will consider in what follows the sheaves (of abelian groups) with basis T𝑐. The
very particular nature of the locally compact totally disconnected topology gives them a very
simple structure, as shown by the following proposition.
Proposition. Let 𝑋be a t.d. space and let F be a sheaf (of abelian groups) with basis T𝑐.
Then there exists an abelian group F𝑐(𝑋) and a sheaf F1 isomorphic to F such that
1. for all 𝑈∈T𝑐, F1(𝑈) ⊂F𝑐(𝑋),
2. if 𝑈⊂𝑉are open sets in T𝑐, then F1(𝑈) ⊂F1(𝑉),
3. if 𝑈⊂𝑉are open sets in T𝑐, then for all 𝑓∈F1(𝑈), 𝜌𝑉,𝑈( 𝑓) = 𝑓while 𝜌𝑉,𝑉\𝑈( 𝑓) = 0,
4. F𝑐(𝑋) = Ð

Proof. Since F is a sheaf, F is isomorphic to F +, the sheaf of sections of the étale space bF .
Let F𝑐(𝑋) be the space of compactly supported sections in F +(𝑋). If 𝑈is a compact open set of
𝑋, we embed F +(𝑈) into F𝑐(𝑋) by extending the sections 𝑠∈F +(𝑈) by 0 outside of 𝑈. Since
𝑋\ 𝑈is open, it is clear that this indeed defines a section in F +(𝑋), and it is supported in 𝑈,
hence compact. The other assertions of the proposition are then easily verified.
□
Example. Let 𝒞∞
𝑋be the sheaf whose space of sections over an open set 𝑈is the space 𝒞∞(𝑈)
of locally constant functions on 𝑈. The abelian group given by the proposition is in this case

therefore (𝑋, 𝒞∞

unless 𝑋is compact, in which case 𝒞∞(𝑋) = 𝒟(𝑋), whose identity is 𝜒𝑋. On the other hand,
it is an algebra with idempotents (see I.1), since each compact open set 𝑈of 𝑋gives an
idempotent 𝜒𝑈and these 𝜒𝑈form a directed system of idempotents (see Remark I.1.2, 2).

Our goal is now to prove the following theorem. Recall that 𝒟(𝑋) is an algebra with
idempotents, with the {𝜒𝑈}, 𝑈compact open, as a system of idempotents. The algebra 𝒞∞(𝑋),
on the other hand, is a unital algebra, with identity 𝜒𝑋. The notion of a non-degenerate
module defined in the previous chapter for algebras with idempotents easily extends to the
case of an algebra equipped with a directed system of idempotents (cf. Remark 3, I.1.2) and
𝒞∞(𝑋) also admits the {𝜒𝑈}, 𝑈compact open, as a directed system of idempotents. In what
follows, we consider non-degenerate modules over 𝒞∞(𝑋) relative to this directed system of
idempotents.
Theorem. For any M ∈𝒞∞
𝑋−M𝑜𝑑(𝑋), M𝑐(𝑋) is a non-degenerate 𝒟(𝑋)-module and
M ↦→M𝑐(𝑋) realizes an equivalence of categories between 𝒞∞
𝑋−M𝑜𝑑(𝑋) and M(𝒟(𝑋)).
Note first that we can replace 𝒟(𝑋) in the statement by 𝒞∞(𝑋).
Lemma. Let 𝑀be a non-degenerate 𝒟(𝑋)-module. Then the action of 𝒟(𝑋) on 𝑀extends

II.3.2
A finiteness result
In this paragraph, 𝐺is a t.d. group. We will often use, without further comment, the following
result:
Lemma. (𝑖) Let 𝐾⊂𝐾1 be compact open subgroups of 𝐺. Then [𝐾1 : 𝐾] is finite.
(𝑖𝑖) If 𝐾is a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, then 𝐺/𝐾equipped with the quotient topology
is discrete. If moreover 𝐺is 𝜎-compact, then 𝐺/𝐾is countable.
Proof. (𝑖) The group 𝐾1 is the disjoint union of right cosets modulo 𝐾, which forms a cover of
𝐾1 by disjoint open sets. By compactness, we can extract a finite subcover. But this subcover
cannot be strictly smaller. Therefore, the set of right cosets is finite.
(𝑖𝑖) Suppose that 𝐺is a countable union of compact subsets 𝐴𝑛, 𝑛∈N. From the cover
of 𝐴𝑛by the open sets 𝑔𝐾, 𝑔∈𝐺, we extract a finite subcover, and thus 𝐺is covered by a
countable family (𝑔𝑖𝐾)𝑖, which shows that 𝐺/𝐾is at most countable.
□

When considering an action of a t.d. group 𝐺on a t.d. space 𝑋, it is always a continuous left

𝐺→𝑋0, 𝑔↦→𝑔· 𝑥0 is open. It follows that all orbits are locally closed in 𝑋.

II.3.6
Haar measure
We show here the existence of a (left) Haar measure on a t.d. group. The result remains valid
for any locally compact topological group, but the proof for t.d. groups given here is simpler
than that of the general case.
Proposition. Let 𝐺be a t.d. group. There exists, up to a scalar multiplicative factor, a
unique distribution 𝜇𝐺on 𝐺invariant under left translation. We can choose 𝜇𝐺to be positive
(i.e., such that ⟨𝜇𝐺, 𝑓⟩> 0 for any non-identically zero function 𝑓∈𝒟(𝐺) with non-negative
values). A right-invariant measure 𝜈𝐺can be defined similarly.
Proof. Let 𝐾𝛼be a fundamental system of neighborhoods of the identity element in 𝐺consisting
of compact open subgroups, and suppose moreover that one of them, say 𝐾𝛼0, contains all
the others. For all 𝛼, let us set 𝒟𝛼= 𝒟(𝐺, 𝐾𝛼, 𝑟). It is clear that if 𝐾𝛼⊂𝐾𝛽, then 𝒟𝛽⊂𝒟𝛼
and that 𝒟(𝐺) = Ð
𝛼𝒟𝛼. On the other hand, each 𝒟𝛼is stable under the action 𝑙of 𝐺. To
define a left-invariant distribution on 𝐺, it suffices to define in a compatible way for each 𝛼
a left-invariant element 𝜇𝛼of the dual of 𝒟𝛼, i.e., if 𝒟𝛽⊂𝒟𝛼, then 𝜇𝛼|𝒟𝛽= 𝜇𝛽. Since 𝒟𝛼
is generated by the left translates of the characteristic function of 𝐾𝛼, this element of the
dual is completely determined by its value on 𝜒𝐾𝛼. It follows that 𝜇𝐺is indeed unique up to
multiplication by a scalar factor. To prove existence, we must normalize the 𝜇𝛼in a compatible
way, and for this, we use the compact open subgroup 𝐾𝛼0 above. We choose 𝜇𝛼0 by setting
⟨𝜇𝛼0, 𝜒𝐾𝛼0 ⟩= 1, and we then normalize the 𝜇𝛼by setting
⟨𝜇𝛼, 𝜒𝐾𝛼⟩= [𝐾𝛼0 : 𝐾𝛼]−1.
It is easy to verify that this indeed defines a compatible family {𝜇𝛼}.
□

Definition. A group 𝐺such that 𝛿𝐺is identically equal to 1 is said to be unimodular.
Examples.
1. An abelian group is unimodular.
2. A compact group is unimodular (because the only compact subgroup of R× is {1}).
3. If 𝐺is the union of its compact subgroups, then 𝐺is unimodular.

We will slightly change the notation introduced in paragraph II.3.5.

Proof. Let 𝑇be a distribution in ℰ′
𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑓(𝐺, 𝑙), and let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup such that 𝑇

form 𝑇= 𝑓𝜇𝐺for a function invariant under the action of 𝐾by 𝑙. Such a function is locally
constant. Moreover, clearly, Supp𝑇= Supp 𝑓and therefore since 𝑇is compactly supported, 𝑓

If Γ is a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, then 𝑒Γ is in H (𝐺). On the other hand, for any 𝑔∈𝐺,
𝛿𝑔is in ℰ′(𝐺) but not in H (𝐺).
Remarks.
1. It is clear that H (𝐺) is stable under the convolution product. The algebra (H (𝐺), ∗) is
not unital.
2. The subalgebra H (𝐺) is in fact a two-sided ideal of (ℰ′(𝐺), ∗). Indeed,
𝑇∗𝑓𝜇𝐺= (𝑇∗𝑓)𝜇𝐺,
𝑓𝜈𝐺∗𝑇= ( 𝑓∗𝑇)𝜈𝐺,
(𝑇∈ℰ′(𝐺)), ( 𝑓∈𝒟(𝐺)).

𝑖𝐾𝑖, which is indeed a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, and we have 𝑒𝐾∗𝑇𝑖∗𝑒𝐾= 𝑇𝑖. It

II.4
Notes on Chapter II
Most of the results in this chapter are in [4]. The section on sheaves on t.d. topological
spaces is largely inspired by the treatment given in [16]. The description of the center and
the completion of H (𝐺) in terms of distributions is in [24], but the notes from a talk by A.
Moy and remarks by M. Tadić were very useful to me.


## Page 63


CHAPTER III
Representations of totally
disconnected groups
Having defined locally compact totally disconnected topological groups in the previous chapter,
we now tackle the representation theory of these groups. We only consider representations
in complex vector spaces, for which it turns out that the good category to consider is that of
smooth representations, i.e., those which are continuous when the representation space is
equipped with the discrete topology. This category is then equivalent to that of non-degenerate
modules over the Hecke algebra of the group defined in Chapter II. This allows us to use
the constructions and results of Chapter I. We deduce from it, for example, directly the
properties of the functor associating to a representation the space of its vectors fixed by a
compact open subgroup, or those of the duality functor (contragredient representation). The
core of the chapter is devoted to the induction and forgetful functors between categories of
representations of two t.d. groups defined by a morphism between these two groups. Here
again, we particularize the results of Chapter I. The most important case is that where the
morphism is an inclusion of a closed subgroup. We recover in this case the usual notion
of induced representation, "Frobenius reciprocity" being the fact that induction is the right
adjoint of the forgetful functor. We also identify in this framework compact induction and the
functor 𝑃of Chapter I, up to a modular character, compact induction being therefore the left
adjoint of the pseudo-forgetful functor. This is particularly useful when the pseudo-forgetful
functor is naturally isomorphic to the forgetful functor, for example when the subgroup is
open. Another important type of functor between categories of representations is given by the
construction of the space of "coinvariants". In a particular case which will be used in Chapter
VI for the construction of the parabolic induction of reductive groups, the Jacquet functor is a
functor of type 𝑃, hence left adjoint to the pseudo-forgetful functor (which is isomorphic to
the forgetful functor in this case).
Another fundamental tool of representation theory is Schur’s lemma. The version we need
here is proved in the appendix. Irreducible representations admit a central character. More
generally, the center of the category of smooth representations acts on irreducible representa-
tions by scalars. We also show that there are enough irreducible smooth representations in the
sense that irreducible representations "separate the points" of the Hecke algebra of the group.
To summarize, our object of study in this chapter is not so much the category of smooth
representations of a totally disconnected group as the functors that can be defined between
such categories. These functors are particular cases of the induction and forgetful functors
of Chapter I, via the equivalences of categories with non-degenerate modules over Hecke
algebras.

III.1
Smooth representations
III.1.1
Generalities
Let 𝐺be a group. A representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺is the data of a complex vector space 𝑉and a
group morphism 𝜋from 𝐺to GL(𝑉). This defines a linear action of 𝐺on 𝑉. A representation
(𝜋, 𝑉) is said to be unitary if 𝑉is equipped with a Hermitian inner product (antilinear with
respect to the first variable) ⟨. , . ⟩𝑉satisfying
⟨𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣, 𝑤⟩𝑉= ⟨𝑣, 𝜋(𝑔−1) · 𝑤⟩𝑉,
(𝑔∈𝐺), (𝑣, 𝑤∈𝑉).
If 𝑊is a subspace of 𝑉stable under the action of 𝐺given by 𝜋, we denote by 𝜋𝑊the
morphism from 𝐺to GL(𝑊) induced by 𝜋. The representation (𝜋𝑊, 𝑊) (often simply denoted
(𝜋, 𝑊)) is a subrepresentation of (𝜋, 𝑉). Similarly, 𝜋induces a morphism 𝜋𝑉/𝑊from 𝐺to
GL(𝑉/𝑊). The representation (𝜋𝑉/𝑊, 𝑉/𝑊) (often simply denoted (𝜋, 𝑉/𝑊)) is a quotient
representation of (𝜋, 𝑉). More generally, if 𝑊1 ⊂𝑊2 are two subspaces of 𝑉stable under the
action of 𝐺, the morphism 𝜋𝑊2/𝑊1 from 𝐺to GL(𝑊2/𝑊1) induced by 𝜋defines a representation
(𝜋𝑊2/𝑊1, 𝑊2/𝑊1) (or simply (𝜋, 𝑊2/𝑊1)) which is said to be a subquotient of (𝜋, 𝑉).
A representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺with 𝑉of dimension 1 is called a character. We can in this
case identify 𝑉with C, and we then sometimes denote this character by C𝜋. If it takes values
in the complex numbers of modulus 1, it is said to be unitary.
Definition. Let 𝐺be a t.d. group. A representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺in a complex vector space
𝑉is said to be smooth, if for all 𝑣in 𝑉, there exists an open subgroup of 𝐺which fixes 𝑣.
Remark. Let us equip 𝑉with the discrete topology. If we assume that for all 𝑣in 𝑉, the map:
𝜙𝑣: 𝐺→𝑉,
𝑔↦→𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣
is continuous, then the inverse image of the open set {𝑣} of 𝑉is an open set, which shows that
𝜋is smooth. Conversely, if (𝜋, 𝑉) is smooth, the inverse image of any open set of 𝑉, which is
the union (composed of open sets) of its points, is a union of open sets, hence an open set.
If (𝜋, 𝑉) is any representation of 𝐺, let 𝑉0 denote the set of vectors of 𝑉fixed by an
open subgroup of 𝐺. It is clear that 𝑉0 is a subspace of 𝑉stable under the action of 𝐺. The
representation (𝜋, 𝑉0) is then trivially smooth. It is called the smooth part of (𝜋, 𝑉).

III.1.4
An equivalence of categories
Any smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺induces an H (𝐺)-module structure on 𝑉.
Theorem. If (𝜋, 𝑉) is a smooth representation of 𝐺, the H (𝐺)-module 𝑉is non-degenerate.
Conversely, any non-degenerate H (𝐺)-module 𝑉defines a smooth representation of 𝐺. These
two operations are inverse to each other and induce an equivalence of categories between
M(𝐺) and M(H (𝐺)).
Proof. Let 𝑣∈𝑉, and let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup of 𝐺which fixes 𝑣. Then 𝜋(𝑒𝐾) · 𝑣= 𝑣
and therefore 𝑣is fixed by an idempotent of H (𝐺). This shows that the H (𝐺)-module 𝑉is
non-degenerate. Moreover, any morphism in M(𝐺) is obviously a morphism in M(H (𝐺)).

Conversely, if 𝑉is a non-degenerate H (𝐺)-module, for all 𝑣∈𝑉, there exists a compact open
subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺such that 𝑒𝐾· 𝑣= 𝑣. If 𝑇∈ℰ′(𝐺), we define 𝑇· 𝑣:= (𝑇∗𝑒𝐾) · 𝑣. This does not
depend on the choice of 𝐾, because this construction is a particular case of the construction
of Lemma I.1.7: indeed any distribution 𝑇∈ℰ′(𝐺) defines an operator ℎ↦→𝑇∗ℎin H (𝐺)
which commutes with right convolution in H (𝐺). Lemma I.1.7 then allows us to functorially
define 𝜋(𝑇) : 𝑉→𝑉. We can then set 𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣:= 𝜋(𝛿𝑔) · 𝑣, 𝑣∈𝑉. It is also clear that any
morphism in M(H (𝐺)) induces a morphism in M(𝐺). The two functors thus defined establish
an equivalence of categories.
□
Remark. By virtue of this equivalence of categories, we will denote the action of an element 𝑇
of H (𝐺) on a vector 𝑣of a smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺, either by 𝜋(𝑇) · 𝑣, or simply by
𝑇· 𝑣. This latter notation will be preferred when the underlying representation 𝜋is clearly
identified by the context.
In Appendix A.VI.3, we recall the notion of objects of finite length and of composition
series (also called Jordan-Hölder series) in certain abelian categories. We can apply this to
modules in M(H (𝐺)) and by the equivalence of categories between M(H (𝐺)) and M(𝐺),
we transpose the terminology of A.VI.3 to smooth representations of 𝐺.
Thus, any smooth representation of 𝐺always admits an irreducible subquotient, if it is
finitely generated, it admits an irreducible quotient, and finally if it is of finite length, it admits
an irreducible subrepresentation.

Chapter III. Representations of t.d. groups
III.1.7
Admissible representations
The admissibility condition of a representation is a technical finiteness criterion allowing one
to obtain many important results. We will show later that in the case of a 𝑝-adic reductive
group, all irreducible smooth representations are admissible.
Definition. A smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of the t.d. group 𝐺is said to be admissible, if
for any open subgroup 𝑁of 𝐺, the subspace of fixed vectors 𝑉𝑁is finite-dimensional.
Let us translate this: since the compact open subgroups form a neighborhood basis of the
identity in 𝐺, the representation (𝜋, 𝑉) is admissible if and only if 𝑉𝐾is finite-dimensional for
any compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺. In terms of an H (𝐺)-module, this means that 𝑒𝐾· 𝑉is
finite-dimensional for any 𝐾as above. But for any idempotent 𝑒of H (𝐺), there exists such a 𝐾
such that 𝑒≤𝑒𝐾. We therefore have 𝑒· 𝑉= 𝑒𝐾𝑒· 𝑉⊂𝑒𝐾· 𝑉, and we see that the representation
(𝜋, 𝑉) is admissible if and only if the non-degenerate H (𝐺)-module 𝑉is admissible in the
sense of I.5.1
Proposition. A smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺is admissible if and only if 𝑉≃ee𝑉.
Proof. This is the translation of Proposition I.5.1. Corollary I.5.1 can also be stated in terms
of representations rather than H (𝐺)-modules.
□
III.1.8
Schur’s Lemma
Proposition. Let 𝐺be a 𝜎-compact t.d. group, and let (𝜋, 𝑉) be an irreducible smooth
representation of 𝐺. Then the space Hom𝐺(𝑉, 𝑉) is the set of scalar operators. If (𝜏, 𝑊) is
another irreducible smooth representation of 𝐺not equivalent to (𝜋, 𝑉), then Hom𝐺(𝑉, 𝑊) =
0.
Proof. Let us first show that 𝑉is of at most countable dimension: let us fix a non-zero 𝑣∈𝑉
and let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup of 𝐺fixing 𝑣. Then by irreducibility, 𝑉is generated
by the vectors 𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣, where 𝑔runs through a system of representatives of 𝐺/𝐾. But such a
system of representatives is countable (Lemma II.3.2).
The proposition then follows from Theorem B.II and the equivalence of categories III.1.4:
Hom𝐺(𝑉, 𝑉) ≃EndH(𝐺) (𝑉).
The second point is obvious, since a non-zero intertwining operator between 𝑉and 𝑊is
necessarily an isomorphism, which is excluded by hypothesis.
□
Remark. If in the proposition, we assume (𝜋, 𝑉) is admissible, we can replace the hypothesis that
𝐺is 𝜎-compact by the fact that the identity element of 𝐺admits a countable neighborhood basis.
Indeed, 𝑉is of countable dimension, since 𝑉= Ð
𝐾𝑉𝐾, where 𝐾runs through a (countable)
family of compact open subgroups of 𝐺forming a neighborhood basis of the identity, and
each 𝑉𝐾is finite-dimensional.
III.1.9
An application of Schur’s Lemma
The following result is an application of Schur’s Lemma that we will use later. We therefore
still assume 𝐺is 𝜎-compact.

Remark. We defined in I.1.7 the center of the category M(H (𝐺)) as being the algebra of
endomorphisms of the identity functor. Let us denote it ℨ(𝐺). It is clear that an element of
𝑍= 𝑍(𝐺) defines, by its action on the modules of M(𝐺) ≃M(H (𝐺)), such an endomorphism,
and therefore an element of ℨ(𝐺). But, for any element 𝑧∈ℨ(𝐺), Schur’s Lemma asserts that
the action of 𝑧on a simple module 𝑉is a scalar operator. As above, this therefore defines for
any simple module 𝑉a character of ℨ(𝐺), which is still called the central character of the
module 𝑉and which extends the character of 𝑍(𝐺) described above.

smooth representations, in the following sense.

Scholium. Let 𝐴1 and 𝐴2 be unital algebras and let (𝜏1, 𝑉1), (𝜏2, 𝑉2) be finite-dimensional

III.1.15
The functors ⊗and Hom
In the previous section, we defined the tensor product of representations of M(𝐺). If we fix a
representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of M(𝐺), we can then define a functor
• ⊗𝑉: M(𝐺) →M(𝐺),
(𝜌, 𝑊) ↦→(𝜌⊗𝜋, 𝑊⊗𝑉).
We will define a Hom functor in a similar way. If (𝜌, 𝑊) and (𝜋, 𝑉) are two representations of
𝐺(not necessarily smooth), Hom(𝑊, 𝑉) = HomC(𝑊, 𝑉) is equipped with a representation 𝜋𝜌
of 𝐺given by: for all 𝑤∈𝑊, for all 𝑔∈𝐺and all 𝜙∈Hom(𝑊, 𝑉),

Even if 𝜌and 𝜋are smooth, the representation thus obtained is not necessarily so. To be able
to define a functor from the category M(𝐺) to itself, we must therefore take its smooth part
Hom(𝑊, 𝑉)0. We still denote by 𝜋𝜌the restriction of 𝜋𝜌to the smooth part. Thus we obtain a
functor:
Hom(𝑊, • )0 : M(𝐺) →M(𝐺),
(𝜋, 𝑉) ↦→(𝜋𝜌, Hom(𝑊, 𝑉)0)

Hom( • , 𝑉)0 : M(𝐺) →M(𝐺),

Lemma. Let (𝜋𝑖, 𝑉𝑖), 𝑖= 1, 2 be representations of 𝐺(not necessarily smooth). Then,
Hom((𝑉1)0, 𝑉2)0 = Hom((𝑉1)0, (𝑉2)0)0.
Proof. Let 𝜙∈Hom((𝑉1)0, 𝑉2)0, 𝑣1 ∈(𝑉1)0. Let us choose a compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺
small enough to simultaneously fix 𝜙and 𝑣1. Then for all 𝑘∈𝐾,
𝜋2(𝑘−1) · 𝜙(𝑣1) = 𝜋2(𝑘−1) · ((𝑘· 𝜙)(𝜋1(𝑘) · 𝑣1))
=𝜋2(𝑘−1) · (𝜋2(𝑘) · (𝜙(𝜋1(𝑘−1) · (𝜋1(𝑘) · 𝑣1)))) = 𝜙(𝑣1).
This shows that 𝜙(𝑣1) is also fixed by 𝐾.

By taking the 𝐺-invariant morphisms on each side of the equality established by the
proposition, we obtain
Corollary. Hom𝐺(𝑉1 ⊗𝑉2, 𝑉3) ≃Hom𝐺(𝑉1, Hom(𝑉2, 𝑉3)0).
Remarks.
1. The corollary shows that in particular the functor • ⊗𝑉2 is the left adjoint of the functor
Hom(𝑉2, • )0. But the proposition also shows naturality in the variable 𝑉2.
2. The above adjunction implies that the functor •⊗𝑉preserves colimits, hence in particular
is right exact and preserves inductive limits, while the functor Hom(𝑉, • )0 preserves
limits, hence in particular is left exact and preserves projective limits.
3. If (𝜔, C𝜔) is a 1-dimensional representation of 𝐺, then • ⊗C𝜔realizes an equivalence
of categories with inverse • ⊗C𝜔−1. Moreover these two functors are adjoints, in both
possible directions, i.e., for any smooth representations (𝜋, 𝑉) and (𝜌, 𝑊) of 𝐺, we have
a natural isomorphism
Hom𝐺(𝑉, 𝑊⊗C𝜔) ≃Hom𝐺(𝑉⊗C𝜔−1, 𝑊),
the other adjunction being obtained by exchanging the roles of 𝜔and 𝜔−1. This shows in
this case that the functor • ⊗C𝜔is a right and left adjoint and therefore that it preserves
limits and colimits. In particular it is exact.

## Page 91


III.4. Notes on Chapter III
85
III.4
Notes on Chapter III
Most of this chapter is taken from [4]. We have added some elements inspired by the
philosophy of [35], which consists of viewing the induction and Jacquet functors as particular
cases of the forgetful (F ,ˇF ) or induction (𝐼, 𝑃) functors defined in the framework of algebras
with idempotents. Beyond the conceptual gain, this allows one to quickly determine the
adjunctions between composites of such functors. The part on representations in spaces of
sections of equivariant sheaves is taken from [5].


## Page 93


CHAPTER IV
Compact, square-integrable, and
unitary representations
We will now study particular classes of smooth representations of a t.d. group. We begin
by recalling the representation theory of compact groups, a theory which, in the framework
of t.d. groups, is particularly elementary. The main result is that the category of smooth
representations of a compact t.d. group is semisimple. This is not the case in general if the
group 𝐺is not compact, but it is then possible to define a class of representations having good
properties, by a support condition on their matrix coefficients: the compact representations.
Any compact representation is semisimple, and if we fix an isomorphism class 𝜏of irreducible
compact representations, any smooth representation of 𝐺decomposes into a direct sum where
the first factor is a direct sum of representations in the class 𝜏, and the second is a smooth
representation of which no irreducible subquotient is in 𝜏. This is interpreted in terms of
a decomposition of categories. We give a simple finiteness criterion for the category M(𝐺)
to decompose into a "compact part", which is a product, over the isomorphism classes 𝜏of
compact representations, of the categories M(𝐺)𝜏whose elements are the direct sums of
representations in the class 𝜏, and a non-compact part, whose elements are the representations
having no compact subquotient. This finiteness criterion will be verified for 𝑝-adic reductive
groups, which is the basis of the Bernstein decomposition theorem. We then study unitary
representations and we introduce a twisted version of the induction functors which preserves
the unitarity of representations. Admissible unitary representations are semisimple. Finally we
look at smooth representations whose central character is unitary and whose matrix coefficients
are square-integrable functions (modulo the center). Such a representation is admissible and
unitary and the coefficients of such representations satisfy the Schur orthogonality formulas.
IV.1
Compact Representations
IV.1.1
Representations of Compact Groups
Let 𝐾be a compact t.d. group.
Theorem. (𝑖) Any irreducible smooth representation of 𝐾is finite-dimensional.
(𝑖𝑖) For any finite-dimensional smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐾, there exists a normal
compact open subgroup 𝑁in 𝐾such that 𝑁acts trivially on 𝑉.
(𝑖𝑖𝑖) Any smooth representation of 𝐾is semisimple.
87


## Page 94


88
Chapter IV. Compact, unitary representations...
(𝑖𝑣) Any smooth representation of 𝐾is unitary.
Proof. (𝑖) Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be an irreducible smooth representation of 𝐾, and let 𝑣∈𝑉be non-zero.
Since 𝜋is smooth, there exists a compact open subgroup 𝐾𝑣of 𝐾which fixes 𝑣. Since 𝐾/𝐾𝑣is
finite (cf. Lemma II.3.2), the vector space 𝑊generated by 𝐾· 𝑣is finite-dimensional. Since it
is 𝐾-stable and non-zero, by the irreducibility of 𝜋, 𝑊= 𝑉.
(𝑖𝑖) Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be a finite-dimensional smooth representation of 𝐾. Let 𝑁= ker 𝜋. It is a
normal subgroup of 𝐾. Let {𝑣𝑖}𝑖=1,...,𝑛be a basis of 𝑉. Then
𝑁=
𝑛
Ù
𝑖=1
Stab𝐾(𝑣𝑖).
Since 𝜋is smooth, each Stab𝐾(𝑣𝑖) is an open subgroup of 𝐾, so 𝑁is open. Since 𝐾is compact,
any open subgroup of 𝐾, being closed, is compact.
(𝑖𝑖𝑖) Let us first show that 𝑉is a sum of irreducible subrepresentations. Let 𝑣∈𝑉and let 𝑊
be the subspace generated by 𝐾· 𝑣. As in the proof of (𝑖), we see that 𝑊is finite-dimensional,
and therefore according to (𝑖𝑖), there exists a normal compact open subgroup 𝑁in 𝐾such

IV.1.2
An Admissibility Criterion
From the above, we derive a necessary and sufficient condition for a smooth representation of
a t.d. group to be admissible.
Proposition. Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of a t.d. group 𝐺. Then (𝜋, 𝑉) is
admissible if and only if for any compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺, any class 𝜎∈b𝐾has finite
multiplicity in Res𝐺
𝐾(𝜋, 𝑉).
Proof. Suppose that for a certain compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺, and a certain class 𝜎∈b𝐾,
the multiplicity 𝑚(𝜎) is not finite. Let 𝑁be a normal compact open subgroup of 𝐾such that
𝑁⊂ker 𝜎. Then dim𝑉𝑁is not finite and therefore (𝜋, 𝑉) is not admissible. Conversely if 𝐾
is a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, the trivial representation of 𝐾being irreducible, dim𝑉𝐾is
finite.
□
IV.1.3

Let 𝐺be a t.d. group. If 𝐺is not compact, the category M(𝐺) is generally not semisimple.
Nevertheless, there exists a class of smooth representations of 𝐺which behave like the
representations of compact groups. In particular they are semisimple.

Compact representations of a group 𝐺do not always exist. A necessary condition is given
in the following lemma.
Lemma. Let 𝐺be a 𝜎-compact t.d. group, and suppose that there exists a compact represen-
tation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺. Then the center of 𝐺is compact.

(𝜋, 𝑉) admits a central character 𝜒𝜋. Let 𝑣∈𝑉be non-zero and 𝐾a compact open subgroup

The support of 𝑓𝐾,𝑣contains 𝑍(𝐺), which is therefore compact.
□

injective. By definition, 𝑒𝐾,𝜏is in the image of 𝜙, and therefore H (𝐺) ∗𝑒𝐾,𝜏embeds into Im 𝜙.
Since 𝑊⊗e
𝑊is, as a representation of 𝐺, a direct sum of irreducible representations equivalent
to 𝑊, the same is true for H (𝐺) ∗𝑒𝐾,𝜏. The image of the 𝐺-morphism
H (𝐺) ∗𝑒𝐾,𝜏→𝑉,

is therefore a direct sum of irreducible representations equivalent to 𝑊. This completes the
proof of the fact that Im 𝜋(𝑒𝜏) is a direct sum of representations isomorphic to (𝜏, 𝑊).
The functor:
(IV.1.5.1)
M(𝐺) →M(𝐺),
𝑉↦→𝜋(𝑒𝜏) · 𝑉
is exact. Indeed, it is clearly left exact. Suppose that
𝛽: (𝜋1, 𝑉1) →(𝜋2, 𝑉2)
is a surjective morphism of 𝐺-modules. For all 𝑤∈𝑉2, there exists 𝑣∈𝑉1 such that 𝛽(𝑣) =

This shows the assertion.
We can now show the last assertion of the proposition. Since 𝜋(𝑒𝜏) annihilates any
subquotient of ker 𝜋(𝑒𝜏), by the exactness of the functor above and the fact that 𝜏(𝑒𝜏) is
the identity, no subquotient of ker 𝜋(𝑒𝜏) is isomorphic to (𝜏, 𝑊).
□
Theorem IV.1.4 is now completely proved.
Remarks. 1. The subspace ker 𝜋(𝑒𝜏) is the only 𝐺-invariant complement of Im 𝜋(𝑒𝜏).
2. If (𝜏′, 𝑊′) is another irreducible compact representation of 𝐺, not equivalent to (𝜏, 𝑊),
then:
𝜋(𝑒𝜏) ◦𝜋(𝑒𝜏′) = 𝜋(𝑒𝜏′) ◦𝜋(𝑒𝜏) = 0.
3. It is easy to verify that 𝑉↦→𝜋(𝑒𝜏) · 𝑉defines a natural transformation of the identity
functor of the category M(𝐺) to itself. We can therefore interpret 𝑒𝜏as an element of the
center of M(𝐺).
IV.1.6
Semisimplicity of Compact Representations
Let us now deduce some consequences from the results obtained above.
Corollary. Any compact representation is semisimple.
Proof. According to Lemma A.VII, it suffices to prove that a compact representation is gen-
erated by its irreducible subrepresentations. Let (𝜌, 𝑊) be a compact representation of 𝐺,
and let 𝑊𝑓be the sum of all irreducible subrepresentations of 𝑊. We must therefore show
that 𝑊/𝑊𝑓is zero. Suppose otherwise, and let 𝜋be an irreducible subquotient of 𝑊/𝑊𝑓. It is
a compact representation, since any subquotient of a compact representation is a compact
representation. We then have 𝑒𝜋· (𝑊/𝑊𝑓) ≠0 and therefore 𝑒𝜋· 𝑊is not included in 𝑊𝑓. But
this leads to a contradiction, because by (𝑖𝑖𝑖) of Proposition IV.1.5, 𝑒𝜋· 𝑊⊂𝑊𝑓.
□

Let us introduce some notation:
Notation. Let (𝜏, 𝑊) be an irreducible compact representation of the t.d. group 𝐺. Let M(𝐺)𝜏
denote the full subcategory of M(𝐺) consisting of representations which are a direct sum
of irreducible representations equivalent to (𝜏, 𝑊) and [M(𝐺) \ 𝜏] the full subcategory of
M(𝐺) consisting of representations of which no irreducible subquotient is isomorphic to (𝜏, 𝑊).
More generally, given non-isomorphic irreducible compact representations (𝜏𝑖, 𝑊𝑖), 𝑖= 1, . . . 𝑟,
let [M(𝐺) \ 𝜏1, . . . , 𝜏𝑟] denote the full subcategory of M(𝐺) consisting of representations of
which no irreducible subquotient is isomorphic to one of the (𝜏𝑖, 𝑊𝑖).
Let M(𝐺)𝑐denote the full subcategory of M(𝐺) consisting of representations of which
all irreducible subquotients are compact representations, and M(𝐺)𝑛𝑐the full subcategory
of M(𝐺) consisting of representations of which no irreducible subquotient is a compact
representation. Let Irr(𝐺)𝑐denote the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible compact
representations.
The category M(𝐺)𝑐is therefore semisimple, and any simple module in it is both projective
and injective.
Proposition. Any irreducible compact representation (𝜏, 𝑊) of 𝐺is projective and injective
in M(𝐺).
Proof. Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be in M(𝐺). We have:
Hom𝐺(𝑊, 𝑉) = Hom𝐺(𝑊, 𝜋(𝑒𝜏) · 𝑉),
Hom𝐺(𝑉, 𝑊) = Hom𝐺(𝜋(𝑒𝜏) · 𝑉, 𝑊).
This reduces us to showing that 𝑊is projective and injective in M(𝐺)𝜏, but as we noted above,
this is indeed the case.
□

Hermitian Representations
If 𝑉is a vector space over C, let 𝑉R denote the underlying real vector space and 𝑉the complex

of a vector 𝑣of 𝑉by a complex number 𝜆∈C is defined as ¯𝜆𝑣. In particular, 𝑉R = 𝑉R. If

by ¯𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣= 𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣, 𝑔∈𝐺, 𝑣∈𝑉. We also set (𝜋ℎ, 𝑉ℎ) = (¯˜𝜋, ¯e𝑉). We note that we also have
(𝜋ℎ, 𝑉ℎ) = (˜¯𝜋,e𝑉).
Definition. We say that the representation (𝜋, 𝑉) is Hermitian if there exists a non-
degenerate Hermitian form ⟨. , . ⟩on 𝑉such that for all 𝑣, 𝑤∈𝑉, for all 𝑔∈𝐺
⟨𝑣, 𝑤⟩= ⟨𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣, 𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑤⟩.
Remarks. 1. If (𝜋ℎ, 𝑉ℎ) is isomorphic to (𝜋, 𝑉), then it is Hermitian. Indeed, the canonical
duality between 𝑉and 𝑉ℎgives the existence of such a form. Conversely, if (𝜋, 𝑉) is admissible,
the Hermitian inner product being non-degenerate, it induces an injection of 𝑉into e𝑉. By
passing to the dual, we obtain a surjection of 𝑉= ee𝑉onto (𝑉)∼(note that this is where we use
the hypothesis that 𝑉is admissible). We deduce by conjugation a surjection of 𝑉into e𝑉, which
is equal to the injection 𝑉↩→e𝑉obtained previously. All these morphisms being 𝐺-equivariant,
we obtain 𝑉≃e𝑉as a representation of 𝐺, hence (𝜋ℎ, 𝑉ℎ) ≃(𝜋, 𝑉).

IV.2.2
Unitary Representations
The unitary representations of a locally compact topological group 𝐺are the continuous
representations of 𝐺in a Hilbert space which preserve the Hermitian inner product. In


## Page 103


IV.2. Unitary Representations
97
general, for a t.d. group such representations are not smooth. In a way, smoothness is
incompatible with the completeness of the representation space. We wish here to stay within
the framework of smooth representations, which pushes us to slightly redefine the notion of
unitary representation. The results cited in [4], 4.21 show that the nuance is ultimately not
very important.
Definition. Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of the group 𝐺. It is said to be unitary
if the complex vector space 𝑉is equipped with a 𝐺-invariant (positive definite) Hermitian
inner product ⟨. , . ⟩𝑉, i.e.,
⟨𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑢, 𝜋(𝑔) · 𝑣⟩𝑉= ⟨𝑢, 𝑣⟩𝑉,
(𝑢, 𝑣∈𝑉), (𝑔∈𝐺).
It follows easily from Proposition III.1.9 that if (𝜋, 𝑉) is irreducible admissible unitary, a 𝐺-
invariant positive definite Hermitian inner product on 𝑉is equal to ⟨. , . ⟩𝑉up to multiplication
by a strictly positive real number.
The admissibility hypothesis will allow us to recover the usual properties of unitary
representations in Hilbert spaces.

## Page 109


CHAPTER V
Structure of 𝑝-adic reductive
groups
This chapter is a catalog of results, not all proved here, but referenced as much as possible. We
introduce our main object of study, 𝑝-adic reductive groups. Our exposition, hitherto roughly
"self-contained", ceases to be so, for it is out of the question to treat the theory of algebraic
groups, or even reductive algebraic groups, in this book, especially since this is very well done
in numerous works ([6], [33], [42], [7]). Similarly, we do not feel courageous (or competent)
enough to present Bruhat-Tits theory ([14], [15], [44]). The class of groups we study is that
of the groups of rational points of a connected reductive algebraic group defined over a 𝑝-adic
field. Bruhat-Tits theory constructs the building of such groups, and the action of the group
on its building. We cannot recommend strongly enough that the reader complete this brief
presentation by studying two or three concrete examples of classical groups, starting with the
group 𝐺𝐿(𝑛). These examples are developed in the literature we indicate.
V.1
Non-Archimedean local fields
We refer the reader to [21] for more details concerning non-Archimedean local fields, as well
as for the proofs of the results stated here.
Let F be a non-Archimedean local field equipped with its discrete valuation 𝑣F taking
values in Z ∪{∞}, normalized so that the image of 𝑣F is Z ∪{∞}. Let 𝔒F denote the ring of
integers of F,
𝔒F = {𝑥∈F | 𝑣F(𝑥) ≥0}
and 𝔓F its unique maximal ideal (𝔒F is a local ring),
𝔓F = {𝑥∈F | 𝑣F(𝑥) > 0}.
Let 𝔒×
F denote the group of units of 𝔒F. It is the set of elements of 𝔒F of valuation zero. Let us
fix a uniformizer 𝜛of F, i.e., an element satisfying 𝑣F(𝜛) = 1. We can then write any 𝑥∈F×
uniquely in the form 𝑥= 𝑢𝜛𝑛where 𝑛∈Z and 𝑢∈𝔒×
F .
Let 𝑘be the residue field 𝔒F/𝔓F. It is a finite field, hence isomorphic to F𝑞, for some power
𝑞of a prime number 𝑝. The normalized absolute value on F is given by
|𝑥|F = 𝑞−𝑣F(𝑥),

topological group (for addition). A neighborhood basis of 0 is given by the compact open

V.2
𝑝-adic reductive groups
V.2.1
Linear algebraic groups
We recall some elements of the theory of linear algebraic groups. We refer the reader to [42]
for the notions and proofs of the statements recalled here. We fix a non-Archimedean local
field F as in the previous section.
Let G be a connected linear algebraic group defined over F and let 𝐺denote the group of its
points over F. We identify G and the group of its points over F. We denote respectively by 𝑅(G)
and 𝑅𝑢(G) the radical and the unipotent radical of G. The group 𝑅(G) is a connected solvable
normal algebraic subgroup of G, and maximal for this property. It is unique. Similarly 𝑅𝑢(G)
is the connected unipotent normal algebraic subgroup of G and maximal for this property. If
𝑅𝑢(G) is trivial, the group G is said to be reductive. If 𝑅(G) is trivial, the group G is said to be
semisimple. Let 𝒟G be the derived group of G. It is always defined over F. If G is reductive,
then 𝑅(G) is central in G, 𝒟G is semisimple, G is generated by 𝑅(G) and 𝒟G, and 𝑅(G) ∩𝒟G
is finite. Let 𝑍(G) denote the center of G. If G is reductive, 𝑅(G) is the identity component of
𝑍(G) and the subgroups 𝑅(G) and 𝑍(G) are defined over F. We denote respectively by 𝑅(𝐺)
and 𝑍(𝐺) their points over F.
If G is reductive, a maximal torus in G is its own centralizer. There exist maximal tori
of G defined over F and if F is of characteristic zero, the set of conjugacy classes under 𝐺of
maximal tori of G defined over F is finite. All tori of G defined and split over F and maximal
for this property are conjugate under 𝐺.
A split component of G is a torus defined and split over F, contained in the radical 𝑅(G)
and maximal for this property. If G is reductive, a split component of G is then a maximal
split torus in the center of G. Since two such tori must be conjugate by 𝑅(𝐺) which is central,
we deduce the uniqueness of the split component for a reductive group.
The group G can be algebraically embedded into a group 𝐺𝐿𝑁(F) for some 𝑁, the embedding
being defined over F. The group 𝐺therefore embeds into 𝐺𝐿𝑁(F). Equipped with the induced
topology, 𝐺becomes a totally disconnected topological group, for which the notions and
results of Chapters II and III therefore apply. This topology is independent of the choice of the
embedding into a 𝐺𝐿𝑁(F). Moreover, if G is reductive, 𝐺is unimodular.
V.2.2
Rational characters
Let G be a connected reductive algebraic group defined over F and let 𝑋∗(G) be the group of
its algebraic characters (i.e., the group of algebraic morphisms defined over F from G to the


## Page 111


V.2. 𝑝-adic reductive groups
105
multiplicative group G𝑚). We denote by 𝑋∗(𝐺) the subgroup of 𝑋∗(G) of characters defined
over F, and we call it the group of rational characters of G or of 𝐺. Since the restriction of
such an algebraic character to 𝒟G is trivial because G𝑚is commutative, 𝑋∗(G) is identified
with the group of algebraic characters of G/𝒟G. Since G = 𝑅(G)𝒟G, we have
G/𝒟G ≃𝑅(G)/(𝑅(G) ∩𝒟G).
Now 𝑅(G) is a torus, and the group of its algebraic characters 𝑋∗(𝑅(G)) is a finitely generated,
torsion-free abelian group (a lattice, hence isomorphic to Z𝑟for some 𝑟∈N). The group
𝑅(G) ∩𝒟G being finite, 𝑋∗(G) is a full-rank sublattice of 𝑋∗(𝑅(G)). The group of rational
characters 𝑋∗(𝐺) of 𝐺is a sublattice, possibly trivial, of the lattice 𝑋∗(G).
Let 𝑋∗(G) be the dual group, 𝑋∗(G) := HomZ(𝑋∗(G), Z). Let ⟨·, ·⟩denote the natural
duality between 𝑋∗(G) and 𝑋∗(G) and note that this duality induces an isomorphism 𝑋∗(G) ≃
HomZ(𝑋∗(G), Z).
We also denote by 𝑋∗(𝐺) the lattice HomZ(𝑋∗(𝐺), Z). Similarly 𝑋∗(𝐺) ≃HomZ(𝑋∗(𝐺), Z).
Remarks. Case of tori.
1. If T is an algebraic torus defined over F, we can moreover identify 𝑋∗(T) with the group
of morphisms from G𝑚to T so that if 𝑡∈G𝑚, 𝜙∈𝑋∗(T) and 𝜒∈𝑋∗(T),
𝜒(𝜙(𝑡)) = 𝑡⟨𝜒,𝜙⟩.
The elements of 𝑋∗(T) form a basis of the vector space of polynomial functions on T. The
group algebra F[𝑋∗(T)] is the algebra of polynomial functions on T, and T is identified with
the spectrum of this algebra.
2. A torus T defined over F is said to be anisotropic if 𝑋∗(𝑇) = {0} and split if 𝑋∗(𝑇) = 𝑋∗(T).
Any torus T defined over F admits a unique maximal split subtorus A, and a unique maximal
anisotropic subtorus T𝑎𝑛and T is the almost direct product of A and T𝑎𝑛(this means that they
generate T and that their intersection is finite).

Proposition. The group 0𝐺is an open, closed and normal unimodular subgroup of 𝐺. Any
compact subgroup of 𝐺is contained in 0𝐺. The derived group 𝒟𝐺is contained in 0𝐺.
Proof. According to the lemma, any compact subgroup of 𝐺is in 0𝐺. On the other hand,
ker |𝜒|F is a normal subgroup of 𝐺, and the intersection of normal subgroups is again normal,
so 0𝐺is normal in 𝐺. Since the characters |𝜒|F are smooth, the ker |𝜒|F are closed in 𝐺. It
follows that 0𝐺is closed in 𝐺. Since 0𝐺contains compact open subgroups, it must be open in
𝐺. It is therefore unimodular because 𝐺is. The last assertion follows from the fact that any
character is trivial on the derived group.
□

V.2.7
Inertia classes of irreducible representations
The group of unramified characters X(𝐺) acts on the set of equivalence classes of irreducible
smooth representations of 𝐺, Irr(𝐺) by
(𝜔, 𝜋) ↦→𝜋⊗𝜔,
(𝜔∈X(𝐺)), (𝜋∈Irr(𝐺)).
The stabilizer of 𝜋∈Irr(𝐺) is denoted by X(𝐺)(𝜋), i.e.,
X(𝐺)(𝜋) = {𝜔∈X(𝐺) | 𝜋⊗𝜔≃𝜋}.
Lemma. Let 𝜋∈Irr(𝐺). Then X(𝐺)(𝜋) is finite.
Proof. We retain the notation of the previous section. In particular we recall that X(𝐺) is
identified with the set of characters of the lattice Λ(𝐺), that the latter possesses a finite-index
sublattice Λ(𝐴𝐺), and that we can lift the latter to a subgroup 𝐶𝐴𝐺contained in 𝐴𝐺(hence
in particular central in 𝐺). The group X(𝐺) acts on X(𝐴𝐺) by multiplication, via the natural
restriction of characters X(𝐺) →X(𝐴𝐺). Moreover, for any 𝜋∈Irr(𝐺), Schur’s Lemma gives
us a character of the central group 𝐶𝐴𝐺by which this group acts in the space of 𝜋. This defines
a map
Irr(𝐺) →HomZ(𝐶𝐴𝐺, C×) ≃X(𝐴𝐺)

which is easily seen to be equivariant for the actions of X(𝐺) on Irr(𝐺) and X(𝐴𝐺) described
above. It therefore suffices to show that the action of X(𝐺) on X(𝐴𝐺) admits finite stabilizers.
This stems from the fact that Λ(𝐴𝐺) is of finite index in Λ(𝐺), and therefore that the number
of characters of Λ(𝐺) which are trivial on Λ(𝐴𝐺) is finite.
□
Corollary. The orbits of X(𝐺) in Irr(𝐺) are isomorphic (but non-canonically) to complex
algebraic tori.
Proof. We know that X(𝐺) is equipped with a complex algebraic torus structure (cf. Section
V.2.4), and the quotient of such a complex torus by a finite subgroup is again a complex torus.
The non-canonicity comes from the fact that one must choose a base point.
□
Notation. The orbits of the action of X(𝐺) in Irr(𝐺) are called inertia classes. We denote by
[𝜋] the inertia class of a representation 𝜋and [Irr(𝐺)] the set of inertia classes.
Let 𝐹denote the algebra of polynomial functions on the variety X(𝐺). We saw in V.2.4
that 𝐹≃C[Λ(𝐺)]. Let 𝐷be an inertia class in Irr(𝐺) and (𝜋, 𝑉) a representation in this inertia

invariants of 𝐹under the action of the finite group X(𝐺)(𝜋). It is convenient to say that 𝑓is a
polynomial function on 𝐷if there exists a polynomial function ˜𝑓on X(𝐺) such that
𝑓(𝜋⊗𝜒) = ˜𝑓(𝜒).

exist 𝑓1 and 𝑓2 in 𝐹such that 𝑓2(𝜒) 𝑓(𝜋⊗𝜒) = 𝑓1(𝜒) and 𝑓2(𝜒) ≠0 for all 𝜒∈X(𝐺) such that
𝜋⊗𝜒∈𝑈.
V.3
Parabolic subgroups
We refer the reader to [7] or [42] for the proofs of the results stated in this section. Let G be
a connected reductive algebraic group defined over F and let us set 𝐺= G(F). An algebraic
subgroup (as groups defined over F) P of G is a parabolic subgroup if the variety P\G is
projective. A parabolic subgroup 𝑃of 𝐺is the group of points in F of a parabolic subgroup P
defined over F. We will say that "𝑃is a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺" to express the fact that 𝑃is
the group of F-points of a parabolic subgroup P of G defined over F. We will do the same for
other subgroups of G defined over F.
The radical and the unipotent radical of a parabolic subgroup P of G defined over F are
defined over F.
V.3.1
Let 𝑃be a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺and 𝑁its unipotent radical. Then there exists a (non-
unique) reductive subgroup 𝑀of 𝐺in 𝑃normalizing 𝑁such that 𝑃= 𝑀⋊𝑁. We then say
that 𝑀is a Levi factor of 𝑃and a Levi subgroup of 𝐺. Levi subgroups are obtained in the
following way. Let 𝐴be a split component of 𝑃and set 𝑀= 𝑍(𝐺, 𝐴). Then 𝑀is a Levi factor of
𝑃. Moreover 𝐴is the split component of 𝑀. If 𝑃is given, the choice of a split component of 𝑃
is therefore equivalent to the choice of a Levi factor. The unipotent radical 𝑁acts transitively
by conjugation on the set of split components of 𝑃, and therefore on the set of Levi factors of
𝑃. The Levi subgroups 𝑀of 𝐺are the centralizers of split tori 𝐴in 𝐺such that 𝐴is the split
component of 𝑀. We will often use an expression like "𝑃= 𝑀𝑁is a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺"
to summarize the situation where 𝑃is a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺, 𝑁is its unipotent radical,
and 𝑀a Levi factor. If 𝑀is a Levi subgroup of 𝐺, we denote by P(𝑀) the set of parabolic


## Page 116


110
Chapter V. Structure of 𝑝-adic groups
subgroups of 𝐺admitting 𝑀as a Levi factor. It is a finite set. Let 𝑃∈P(𝑀). We then denote by
𝑃= 𝑀𝑁the opposite parabolic subgroup to 𝑃, i.e., the unique parabolic subgroup 𝑄∈P(𝑀)
such that 𝑃∩𝑄= 𝑀, and 𝐴𝑀(or simply 𝐴) the split component of 𝑀.
V.3.2
The minimal parabolic subgroups of 𝐺are those whose split components are maximal split
tori of 𝐺. Two split tori of 𝐺, maximal for this property, are conjugate in 𝐺. Two minimal
parabolic subgroups of 𝐺are therefore conjugate in 𝐺.
V.3.3
Let 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁be a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺, and 𝐴the split component of 𝑀. We set:
𝔞= 𝑋∗(𝐴) ⊗Z R,
𝔞∗= 𝑋∗(𝐴) ⊗Z R.
The perfect duality between 𝑋∗(𝐴) and 𝑋∗(𝐴) extends to a vector space duality between 𝔞
and 𝔞∗, which is consistent with the notation.
V.3.4
Let 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁be a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺, and 𝐴the split component of 𝑀. Let Σ′(𝐴) denote
the set of roots of 𝐴in Lie(𝐺). Then Σ′(𝐴) is identified with a subset of 𝔞∗. We denote by Σ(𝐴)
the set of reduced roots of Σ′(𝐴), i.e., the elements 𝛼of Σ′(𝐴) such that 𝛼/𝑛∉Σ′(𝐴) if 𝑛≥2.
If 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁∈P(𝑀), we denote by Σ′(𝑃) the system of positive roots in Σ′(𝐴) relative to 𝑃
(the roots of 𝐴in 𝑁) and Σ(𝑃) the system of reduced positive roots.
V.3.5

V.5.5
Proof of Langlands’ combinatorial lemma
Langlands’ combinatorial lemma is based on the properties of projection onto a cone in a
Euclidean space. Let 𝑉be such a space, finite-dimensional, where we denote by ⟨. , . ⟩the
inner product. Let us fix an open cone 𝐶of 𝑉, and denote byˇ𝐶its dual
ˇ𝐶= {𝑣∈𝑉| ∀𝑤∈𝐶, ⟨𝑣, 𝑤⟩> 0}.
Let 𝐶andˇ𝐶denote the respective closures of 𝐶andˇ𝐶.
Proposition. For all 𝑣∈𝑉, there exists a unique element 𝑣0 = 𝑝𝐶(𝑣) of 𝐶, called the
projection of 𝑣onto 𝐶, satisfying
(𝑖) ∀𝑤∈𝐶, ||𝑣−𝑣0|| ≤||𝑣−𝑤||.
Moreover, the element 𝑣0 is characterized by one of the following properties, equivalent
to (𝑖),
(𝑖𝑖) ∀𝑤∈𝐶, ⟨𝑣−𝑣0, 𝑤−𝑣0⟩≤0,
(𝑖𝑖𝑖) the vector 𝑣0 −𝑣is inˇ𝐶and ⟨𝑣−𝑣0, 𝑣0⟩= 0.
The existence of the projection is a standard result for any closed convex subset of a Hilbert
space. The equivalence of conditions (𝑖), (𝑖𝑖) and (𝑖𝑖𝑖) for cones is elementary and left to the
reader.
