## Page 135


CHAPTER VI
Representations of 𝑝-adic reductive
groups
This chapter constitutes the core of the book. It presents the theory of the "Bernstein center",
which gives an explicit description of the center of the category M(𝐺) of smooth representa-
tions of a 𝑝-adic reductive group 𝐺. Defined abstractly, the center of an abelian category A is
the set, equipped with a ring structure, of natural transformations from the identity functor
of the category A to itself. In the case where A is the category of non-degenerate modules
over a C-algebra with idempotents 𝐴, we gave in Chapter I a description of this center as the
center of the completed ring 𝐴. When A = M(𝐺), the category of smooth representations of
a totally disconnected group 𝐺, the equivalence of categories M(𝐺) ≃M(H (𝐺)) established
in Chapter II allows us to give a description of the center of this category as a convolution
algebra of essentially compact invariant distributions. This description is of a geometric nature.
Bernstein’s theory gives another one, of a "spectral" nature. To clarify this concept, it may
be useful to recall what happens for finite groups. If 𝐺is a finite group, the category of
representations of 𝐺is equivalent to the category of unital C[𝐺]-modules, C[𝐺] being the
algebra (over C) of the group 𝐺, which is viewed as the algebra of complex-valued functions
on 𝐺, equipped with the convolution product. In this case, we know that the center of the
category is naturally identified with the center of the ring C[𝐺], i.e., with the algebra C[𝐺]𝐺of
constant functions on the conjugacy classes of 𝐺. Recall there are two natural bases for C[𝐺]𝐺:
one coming from geometry, consisting of the characteristic functions of the conjugacy classes
of 𝐺, and the other, spectral, consisting of the characters of the irreducible representations. We
have seen the analogue of the first of these bases for t.d. groups. The theory of the Bernstein
center gives the analogue for 𝑝-adic reductive groups of the second.
The structure of 𝑝-adic reductive groups recalled in the previous chapter highlights the
fact that such a group possesses remarkable subgroups, the Levi subgroups, which are again
𝑝-adic reductive groups. The idea underlying the entire representation theory of these groups
since its origin is that one can study the representations of the group 𝐺via the parabolic
induction functors, defined by the general constructions of Chapter III, between categories of
smooth representations of Levi subgroups and the category of smooth representations of 𝐺.
We therefore begin by defining these functors, denoted 𝑖𝐺
𝑃, where 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁is a parabolic
subgroup of 𝐺, as a composition of functors studied in Chapter III. We immediately deduce
the existence of a left adjoint, denoted 𝑟𝐺
𝑃, given explicitly (parabolic restriction functor, also
called Jacquet functor), and the existence of a right adjoint. The determination of this right
adjoint as being the Jacquet functor 𝑟𝐺
¯𝑃is a deep result, called Bernstein’s second adjunction
theorem, and will be established much later in the chapter. These functors are normalized so
that 𝑖𝐺
𝑃preserves the unitarity of representations. Moreover these functors are exact, and the
129


## Page 136


130
Chapter VI. Representations of 𝑝-adic groups
induction functors are additionally faithful.
Having defined the induction functors, the notion of supercuspidal representation naturally
emerges. Heuristically, these representations are those that do not come from Levi subgroups
via the induction functors. More precisely, a representation is supercuspidal if its image under
all Jacquet functors 𝑟𝐺
𝑃is zero, where 𝑃runs through the set of proper parabolic subgroups
of 𝐺. An irreducible smooth representation of 𝐺is either supercuspidal, or it appears as a
subrepresentation of an induced representation from a Levi subgroup. Thus a good part
of the representation theory of reductive groups boils down to two problems: the study of
supercuspidal representations and the study of parabolic induction functors.
A first result, Harish-Chandra’s theorem, characterizes supercuspidal representations by a
support condition on their matrix coefficients: their support is compact modulo the center
𝑍(𝐺) of 𝐺. We thus almost recover the definition of compact representations from Chapter
IV, with the nuance introduced by the center of the group. It is therefore important to
understand the consequences of this nuance, and to see which results from the theory of
compact representations can be preserved. First of all, like compact representations, irreducible
supercuspidal representations are admissible. We deduce the admissibility of all irreducible
smooth representations from the fact that parabolic induction functors preserve admissibility.
This result is a fairly immediate generalization of the case of compact representations, but
it can be crucially improved by exploiting the structure of reductive groups via a uniform
admissibility theorem: if 𝐺is a 𝑝-adic reductive group, and 𝐾a compact open subgroup of 𝐺,
there exists a positive constant 𝑐such that the dimension of 𝑉𝐾, for any irreducible smooth
representation 𝑉of 𝐺, is bounded by 𝑐. This fact forms the basis of all subsequent developments
in Bernstein’s theory. It leads to a first decomposition of the category M(𝐺), starting from
the category decompositions obtained in Chapter IV using compact representations. We
must take into account the role played by the center, when it is non-compact, and introduce
an equivalence relation on supercuspidal representations, whose classes are called inertia
classes: two representations are in the same class if they differ by an unramified character
of 𝐺. Since the set of unramified characters of 𝐺is equipped with a complex algebraic
variety structure (it is a torus), and since 𝐷is isomorphic (non canonically, one must choose
a base point) to a quotient of this torus by a finite subgroup, 𝐷also admits a complex
algebraic variety structure, and it is again a torus. Having fixed an inertia class 𝐷of irreducible
supercuspidal representations of 𝐺, any smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) can be written as a direct
sum of a representation all of whose irreducible subquotients are in 𝐷, and a representation
none of whose irreducible subquotients are in 𝐷. But the uniform admissibility theorem,
through the finiteness criterion (KF) of Chapter IV, implies a much finer decomposition of
M(𝐺): any smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) can be written as a direct sum of a supercuspidal
representation, itself a direct sum over the inertia classes 𝐷of representations all of whose
irreducible subquotients are in 𝐷, and a representation none of whose irreducible subquotients
are supercuspidal. This constitutes a first step in Bernstein’s decomposition theorem, which
describes M(𝐺) as a product of indecomposable categories. It remains to decompose the non-
cuspidal part, which we will do by a careful study of the parabolic induction functors. Before
doing so, let us completely describe the supercuspidal factors of the decomposition. Let us fix
an inertia class 𝐷of irreducible supercuspidal representations of 𝐺, and let M(𝐺)𝐷denote the
full subcategory of smooth representations all of whose irreducible subquotients are in 𝐷. This
category admits a small (i.e., finitely generated) progenerator, which we exhibit, and which by
general arguments of category theory, yields an equivalence of categories between M(𝐺)𝐷and
the category of right modules over the endomorphism ring of this progenerator. The structure
of this ring is well understood: it is a C-algebra of Laurent polynomials, slightly twisted, i.e.,
it is commutative only up to multiplication by scalar factors. Furthermore, the center of this
algebra is indeed an algebra of Laurent polynomials. It is, in fact, the algebra of polynomial
functions on the variety 𝐷. An element 𝑧of the center of M(𝐺)𝐷is identified with a function
on 𝐷, and the evaluation of this function at a point 𝜋of 𝐷is the scalar by which, according
to Schur’s lemma, 𝑧acts in the space of the representation 𝜋. This completes the analysis of
the supercuspidal part of M(𝐺). Note incidentally that it follows from the description of the
center of M(𝐺)𝐷as an algebra of Laurent polynomials that it is a Noetherian algebra.

A cuspidal datum is a pair (𝑀, 𝜌) where 𝑀is a Levi subgroup of 𝐺and 𝜌an irreducible
supercuspidal representation of 𝑀. Two cuspidal data are associated if they are conjugate
under 𝐺. If (𝑀1, 𝜌1) and (𝑀2, 𝜌2) are two cuspidal data, and if 𝑃1, 𝑃2 are parabolic subgroups
of 𝐺with Levi factors 𝑀1 and 𝑀2 respectively, then the induced representations 𝑖𝐺

𝑃2 𝜌2 admit a common irreducible subquotient if and only if the cuspidal data (𝑀1, 𝜌1) and
(𝑀2, 𝜌2) are associated. The Jordan-Hölder series of 𝑖𝐺
𝑃1 𝜌1 and 𝑖𝐺
𝑃2 𝜌2 are then equivalent.
We continue the study of the functors 𝑖𝐺
𝑃, 𝑟𝐺
𝑃, by establishing that they preserve certain
classes of representations. It is elementary to show that the functors 𝑖𝐺
𝑃preserve admissibility,
and that the functors 𝑟𝐺
𝑃preserve finite generation. Jacquet’s lemma, in a first version applying
only to admissible representations, shows that the functors 𝑟𝐺
𝑃also preserve admissibility. It is
a simple consequence of the geometric lemma that the functors 𝑖𝐺
𝑃preserve representations of
finite length. Howe’s theorem establishes that a representation is of finite length if and only if
it is finitely generated and admissible. It follows that the functors 𝑟𝐺
𝑃preserve representations
of finite length.
We then continue the study of the induced part of the category M(𝐺). For this, we must
equip the set of equivalence classes (under conjugation) of cuspidal data Ω(𝐺) of 𝐺with
a variety structure. This is inherited from the fact that the inertia classes of irreducible
supercuspidal representations of the Levi factors of 𝐺are equipped with such a structure; we
have even seen that they are complex tori. We must take conjugation into account, and we then
see that Ω(𝐺) is a disjoint union (infinite) of connected components, which are themselves
complex algebraic varieties, more precisely quotients of tori by the action of a finite group.
We denote by B(𝐺) the set of connected components of Ω(𝐺). To each irreducible smooth
representation of 𝐺, we can associate, by the properties of induced representations stated
above, an element of Ω(𝐺) called the cuspidal support (or also, by analogy with the theory of
real groups, infinitesimal character) of the representation, depending only on its isomorphism
class. We can now state Bernstein’s decomposition theorem. For any element 𝔰of B(𝐺) (i.e.,
a connected component of Ω(𝐺)), let M(𝐺)𝔰be the full subcategory of representations of
M(𝐺) all of whose irreducible subquotients have a cuspidal support in 𝔰. Then M(𝐺) is the
product over B(𝐺) of the categories M(𝐺)𝔰. The proof of this theorem relies on the properties
of the induction and restriction functors previously established. As a corollary, we deduce a
Noetherian property of the category M(𝐺), which implies that the functors 𝑖𝐺
𝑃preserve finitely
generated representations.
Following the ideas that guided the analysis of the cuspidal part, we would now like
to analyze each of the components M(𝐺)𝔰, by describing these categories as categories of
modules over a unital algebra, and thereby obtain a description of their centers. To do this,
as for the cuspidal components, we must exhibit a small progenerator of these categories.
A natural approach is to construct it via parabolic induction from a small progenerator of
a cuspidal component 𝐷of a Levi 𝑀such that the pair (𝑀, 𝐷) determines the connected
component 𝔰. We have seen that the induction functors preserve finite generation, which
shows that the object thus obtained is small. A technical result shows that this object is
independent of the choices made for 𝑀and 𝐷, and it follows quite easily that it is indeed
a generator of the category. It remains to show that it is also a projective object. Since 𝑖𝐺
𝑃
admits a right adjoint, a general result tells us that it suffices for this adjoint to be an exact
functor. We stated above that the right adjoint of 𝑖𝐺
𝑃is 𝑟𝐺
¯𝑃, and therefore is exact. The proof of

this result, Bernstein’s second adjunction theorem, is delicate. It relies on two intermediate
results, which will be used again later. The first is the strong version (without admissibility
hypothesis) of Jacquet’s lemma, which asserts that if 𝐾is a compact open subgroup of 𝐺
admitting an Iwahori decomposition 𝐾= 𝐾𝑁𝐾𝑀𝐾𝑁with respect to the standard parabolic
subgroups, then for any smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺, the natural map from 𝑉𝐾to its
Jacquet module 𝑟𝐺
𝑃(𝑉) realizes a surjection onto 𝑟𝐺
𝑃(𝑉)𝐾𝑀. Moreover, we obtain a canonical
section of this surjection. From this, we deduce the second result, which is the existence of a
non-degenerate 𝑀-equivariant duality 1 between the spaces 𝑟𝐺
𝑃(𝑉) and 𝑟𝐺
¯𝑃(e𝑉), for any smooth

equivalent to the second adjunction theorem, and we therefore have explicit progenerators of
the categories M(𝐺)𝔰. From there, we obtain, as for the cuspidal components, a description
of the center of the category M(𝐺)𝔰as an algebra of polynomial functions on the variety 𝔰,
the fact that the categories M(𝐺)𝔰are indecomposable, and the description of the center of
the entire category M(𝐺) as an algebra of polynomial functions on Ω(𝐺).

We denote by M(𝐺)𝑠𝑐the full subcategory of M(𝐺) whose objects are the supercuspidal
representations of 𝐺. Since M(𝐺)𝑠𝑐is stable under passing to subquotients, it is an abelian
category. We denote by Irr(𝐺)𝑠𝑐the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible supercuspidal
representations of 𝐺.
VI.2.2
Admissibility of Irreducible Representations
The relationship established between supercuspidal representations and compact representa-
tions and the properties of parabolic induction allow us to show the following result.
Theorem. Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be an irreducible smooth representation of 𝐺. Then (𝜋, 𝑉) is admissible.
Proof. According to Proposition IV.1.3, any finitely generated compact representation is ad-
missible. Recall, according to Theorem VI.2.1, that an irreducible supercuspidal representation
has a restriction to 0𝐺which is compact. Since the quotient 𝐺/0𝐺𝑍(𝐺) is finite (Proposition
V.2.6), and 𝑍(𝐺) acts by scalars on an irreducible representation according to Schur’s lemma
III.1.8, it is also finitely generated. This restriction is therefore admissible. Since any compact
subgroup of 𝐺is contained in 0𝐺, the admissibility of a representation depends only on its
restriction to 0𝐺. It is therefore clear that an irreducible supercuspidal representation is
admissible. In the general case, by the corollary of the previous section, we can embed (𝜋, 𝑉)
into a representation of the form 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜏, 𝐸), with (𝜏, 𝐸) irreducible supercuspidal, and therefore
admissible. According to Lemma III.2.3, 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜏, 𝐸) is admissible, and therefore (𝜋, 𝑉) is too.
□
Corollary. Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of 𝐺. Then 𝜋is irreducible (resp. irreducible
supercuspidal) if and only if ˜𝜋is irreducible (resp. irreducible supercuspidal).
Proof. This follows from the theorem and Corollary III.1.7.
□

natural integer 𝑙. Defining a Λ-module structure on the vector space 𝑆therefore amounts to
giving 𝑙commuting endomorphisms 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑙of 𝑆. The existence of a subquotient 𝑇such
that 𝑇Λ ≠{0} means that there exist subspaces 𝑆1 ⊂𝑆2 of 𝑆stable under 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑙such that
the endomorphisms induced by 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑙on 𝑇= 𝑆2/𝑆1 are the identity. We deduce that 1 is
a simultaneous eigenvalue of 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑙. In other words 𝑆Λ ≠{0}. The proof of the fact that
Hom𝐺(𝜋, 𝜏) ≠{0} is similar.
□
This result admits a variant: let us fix a (smooth) central character

and denote by M(𝐺)𝜒the full subcategory of M(𝐺) whose objects are the smooth representa-
tions admitting the central character 𝜒.
Proposition. Let (𝜏, 𝑊) be an irreducible supercuspidal representation of 𝐺with central
character 𝜒. Then (𝜏, 𝑊) is projective and injective in M(𝐺)𝜒.
Proof. Let us prove projectivity, the proof of injectivity being similar. We must show that if

of the lemma, we can assume (𝜋, 𝑉) is in M(𝐺)[𝜏]. Since the restrictions of (𝜋, 𝑉) and (𝜏, 𝑊)
to 0𝐺are semisimple, there exists a 0𝐺-equivariant section 𝑠0 : (𝜏, 𝑊) →(𝜋, 𝑉) such that

finite abelian group ¯𝐺= 𝐺/0𝐺𝑍(𝐺) acts on S by
¯𝑔· 𝑠= 𝜋(𝑔) ◦𝑠◦𝜏(𝑔−1).
Let 𝑠0 ∈S be non-zero. Let us set 𝑠= | ¯𝐺|−1 Í

VI.4
The Center of M(𝐺)[𝜋]
We will now study more precisely the category M(𝐺)[𝜋], in particular its center. The definition
of the center of a category is found in A.X.

VI.4.1
Progenerator of M(𝐺)[𝜋]
Throughout this section, we fix an irreducible supercuspidal representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺. Our
goal is to describe the category M(𝐺)[𝜋] as the category of unital right modules over a certain
unital ring. To do this, we will exhibit a small progenerator (in the sense of A.VIII.2) of
M(𝐺)[𝜋]. Theorem A.VIII.2 then ensures that such a description is possible. Since Theorem
A.VIII.2 is stated without proof, and we need an explicit version tailored to our context, we
will provide complete proofs for all results.

We can deduce another important consequence from the above results:
Corollary. The C-algebra A is Noetherian.
Proof. Indeed, Z is isomorphic to a Laurent polynomial algebra in several variables, hence
Noetherian ([25], 3.6.2). On the other hand, A is a finitely generated module over Z. We
conclude by using ([25], 3.6.1). The category M(𝐺)[𝜋] is therefore equivalent to the category
of right modules over a Noetherian algebra.
□
Proposition. For all 𝑔∈𝑇, let 𝜓𝑔denote the element of Z whose support is 0𝐺𝑔−1 normalized
as above, i.e., 𝜓𝑔(𝑔−1) = 𝜌𝑇(𝑔−1). Then the map 𝑔↦→𝜓𝑔induces an isomorphism C[𝑇/0𝐺] ≃
Z. This shows that Z is isomorphic to the algebra of functions of the variety Irr(𝐺)[𝜋].
Proof. The fact that 𝑔↦→𝜓𝑔induces an isomorphism C[𝑇/0𝐺] ≃Z follows easily from
(VI.4.3.2). We noted in VI.4.4 that the algebra of functions on Irr(𝐺)[𝜋] is C[𝑇/0𝐺]. The last
assertion follows from this.
□
To conclude this section, we summarize the results obtained on the center of M(𝐺)[𝜋]. The
equivalence of categories between M(𝐺)[𝜋] and M(A)𝑑shows that the center of M(𝐺)[𝜋] is
isomorphic to Z, the center of A. Furthermore, Z is isomorphic to the algebra of polynomial
functions on the variety Irr(𝐺)[𝜋]. We thus obtain a description of the center of M(𝐺)[𝜋] as
the algebra of polynomial functions on Irr(𝐺)[𝜋]. The above isomorphisms are not canonical:
Irr(𝐺)[𝜋] is described as a homogeneous space for X(𝐺), and this depends on the choice

of a base point (here 𝜋) in the inertia class [𝜋]. On the other hand, we chose a particular
progenerator of M(𝐺)[𝜋], on which the definition of the algebra R depends, and therefore
that of A. In the next section, we will see how to make this description independent of the
choices.
VI.4.5
The Center of M(𝐺)[𝜋]: Conclusion
Let 𝑀be a simple right A-module. The center Z of A acts by a character 𝜆𝑀: Z →C. Let
us set 𝐼𝑀= ker 𝜆𝑀, so that the two-sided ideal 𝐼𝑀A is contained in the annihilator of 𝑀. The
structure morphism
𝜙𝑀: A →EndC(𝑀)
induces an algebra morphism, surjective according to Burnside’s theorem ([36], Corollary
XVII.3.3):
˜𝜙𝑀: A/𝐼𝑀A →EndC(𝑀).
Note that A/𝐼𝑀A is of dimension 𝑚2 over C because A is free over Z of rank 𝑚2, and Z/𝐼𝑀≃C.
Since EndC(𝑀) is also of dimension 𝑚2, we deduce that ˜𝜙𝑀is an algebra isomorphism.
Moreover EndC(𝑀) is isomorphic as a right EndC(𝑀)-module to a sum of 𝑚copies of 𝑀. We
deduce that the same is true for A/𝐼𝑀A. It follows that 𝑀is entirely determined by the action
of Z, or more precisely by the ideal 𝐼𝑀of Z. Conversely, for any maximal ideal 𝐼of Z, the
finitely generated right A-module A/𝐼A admits a simple quotient 𝑀, 𝐼= 𝐼𝑀and A/𝐼A ≃𝑚𝑀.
In summary, if 𝑀𝐼denotes the unique simple composition factor of the semisimple right
A-module A/𝐼A, then
𝐼↦→𝑀𝐼,
SpecMax(Z) →Irr(A).
defines a bijection between the set of maximal ideals of Z and the set of isomorphism classes
of simple right A-modules. This equips Irr(A) with the structure of an affine algebraic variety
over C, with function algebra Z.
The equivalence of categories between M(A)𝑑and M(𝐺)[𝜋] therefore induces an algebra
isomorphism between Z and the center Z[𝜋] of the category M(𝐺)[𝜋], denoted by 𝜙↦→𝑧𝜙.
This equivalence also induces a bijection between Irr(A) and Irr(𝐺)[𝜋].
For all 𝐼∈SpecMax(Z), the module A/𝐼A in M(A)𝑑corresponds to the module
(A/𝐼A) ⊗A Π ≃Π/𝐼Π.
There exists a unique irreducible representation (up to isomorphism) 𝜋𝐼in M(𝐺)[𝜋] such that
Π/𝐼Π ≃𝑚𝜋𝐼. Let 𝜆𝐼denote the algebra morphism from Z to C such that ker 𝜆𝐼= 𝐼. Let 𝜙∈Z.
Then 𝜙acts on 𝑀𝐼by multiplication by 𝜆𝐼(𝜙). We deduce that 𝑧𝜙acts on 𝜋𝐼by this same scalar
𝜆𝐼(𝜙).
Proposition. Let 𝑧∈Z[𝜋] and let 𝜏∈Irr(𝐺)[𝜋]. Let us interpret 𝑧as an element of the
algebra of functions of the affine algebraic variety Irr(𝐺)[𝜋]. Then the evaluation of 𝑧at the
point 𝜏is the scalar 𝑧(𝜏) by which 𝑧acts on 𝜏.
Proof. Let 𝜔∈X(𝐺) and 𝑥∈𝑇. It suffices to prove the assertion in the case where 𝑧= 𝑧𝜓𝑥, 𝜓𝑥
being the element of Z defined in Proposition VI.4.4. According to (VI.4.4.5), the function 𝜓𝑥
acts on the simple right module Hom0𝐺(𝜌, 𝜋⊗𝜔) by the scalar 𝜔(𝑥). Thus, the element 𝑧𝜓𝑥
acts by the scalar 𝜔(𝑥) on 𝜋⊗𝜔.
□
We note another consequence of all this:
Corollary. The category M(𝐺)[𝜋] is indecomposable.

VI.5. Induced representations
157
Proof. When an abelian category splits into a direct sum of two categories, the projections
onto each of the factors are idempotents in the center of the category. Since Z is an integral
domain (according to Corollary VI.4.3), it contains no non-trivial idempotent element.
□

VI.5.2
Cuspidal data
From the geometric lemma VI.5.1, we deduce information about the Jordan-Hölder series of
representations induced from supercuspidal representations. More precisely, let 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁be
a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺, and (𝜌, 𝑊) an irreducible supercuspidal representation of 𝑀. We
want to study the induced representation 𝜋= 𝑖𝐺
𝑃𝜌.
Definition. A cuspidal datum is a pair (𝑀, (𝜌, 𝑊)) where 𝑀is a Levi factor of a parabolic
subgroup 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁of 𝐺and (𝜌, 𝑊) is an irreducible supercuspidal representation of 𝑀.
We say that two cuspidal data (𝑀1, (𝜌1, 𝑊1)) and (𝑀2, (𝜌2, 𝑊2)) are associated if there
exists 𝑔∈𝐺such that
𝑔𝑀1𝑔−1 = 𝑀2
and
𝜌2 ≃𝜌𝑔
1
This defines an equivalence relation on the set of cuspidal data. We denote by
(𝑀, (𝜌, 𝑊))𝐺the class of (𝑀, (𝜌, 𝑊)) and Ω(𝐺) the set of equivalence classes.
Recall that according to Corollary VI.2.1, for any irreducible smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉)
of 𝐺, one can find a parabolic subgroup 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁of 𝐺and an irreducible supercuspidal
representation (𝜏, 𝐸) of 𝑀such that
(VI.5.2.1)
Hom𝑀(𝑟𝐺
𝑃(𝜋, 𝑉), (𝜏, 𝐸)) ≠{0}.
Equivalently, by Frobenius reciprocity
(VI.5.2.2)
Hom𝐺((𝜋, 𝑉), 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜏, 𝐸)) ≠{0}.
The following result "separates" induced representations and supercuspidal representa-
tions:
Lemma. Let 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁be a proper parabolic subgroup of 𝐺and let (𝜌, 𝑊) be a smooth
representation of 𝑀. Set (𝜋, 𝑉) = 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜌, 𝑊). Then no irreducible subquotient of (𝜋, 𝑉) is
supercuspidal.
Proof. Let 𝜋𝑠𝑐be the supercuspidal part of 𝜋in the decomposition of Theorem VI.3.5. We have
Hom𝐺(𝜋𝑠𝑐, 𝜋) = Hom𝑀(𝑟𝐺
𝑃(𝜋𝑠𝑐), 𝜌) = 0
and this implies that 𝜋𝑠𝑐is zero.
□

VI.6
Finiteness theorems
In this section, we show that the functors 𝑖𝐺
𝑃and 𝑟𝐺
𝑃preserve certain properties of repre-
sentations. We already know from Lemma III.2.3 that 𝑖𝐺
𝑃preserves admissibility and from
Proposition VI.1.3 that 𝑟𝐺
𝑃preserves finite generation. We show that 𝑟𝐺
𝑃preserves admissibility
and finite length and that 𝑖𝐺
𝑃preserves finite length. We will show later (VI.7.5) that 𝑖𝐺
𝑃
preserves finite generation.
VI.6.1
Jacquet’s lemma
The "Jacquet lemma" (here promoted to the rank of a theorem) is proved under an admissibility
hypothesis. This hypothesis will be removed later (see Section VI.9.1), and the proof will be
considerably more difficult. The weak version proved here is sufficient to obtain as a corollary
the fact that the functors 𝑟𝐺
𝑃preserve admissibility. The notion of an Iwahori decomposition of
a compact open subgroup and the related notation were introduced in V.5.2.
Theorem. Let 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁be a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺, with split component 𝐴, and let
(𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth admissible representation of 𝐺. Let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup of 𝐺
admitting an Iwahori decomposition with respect to 𝑃. Then the projection map 𝑉→𝑉𝑁
maps 𝑉𝐾surjectively onto (𝑉𝑁)𝐾𝑀.

for all 𝑘∈𝐾𝑀we have:
𝜋𝑁(𝑘) 𝑗(𝑣) = 𝑗(𝜋(𝑘) · 𝑣) = 𝑗(𝑣)
and thus the image of 𝑉𝐾under 𝑗is indeed in (𝑉𝑁)𝐾𝑀. We then choose 𝑡∈𝐶++

VI.6.3
A theorem of Howe
We now want to show the following result:
Theorem. Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of 𝐺. Then (𝜋, 𝑉) is of finite length if and
only if it is finitely generated and admissible.

Chapter VI. Representations of 𝑝-adic groups
Proposition. Let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, contained and normal in 𝐾0 and let
(𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of 𝐺. If 𝑉𝐾generates 𝑉, then any non-zero supercuspidal
subquotient of 𝑉contains non-zero vectors fixed by 𝐾.
Proof. Let (𝜏, 𝐸) be an irreducible supercuspidal representation of 𝐺. We use (VI.3.3.1)
to decompose 𝑉into a direct sum of subrepresentations 𝑉= 𝑉(𝜏) ⊕𝑊, where 𝑉(𝜏) is a
representation all of whose irreducible subquotients are in the inertial class [𝜏], and where
no irreducible subquotient of 𝑊is in [𝜏]. Since 𝑉is generated by 𝑉𝐾, the same is true for
𝑉(𝜏). Consider the restriction of (𝜏, 𝐸) to 0𝐺. It is a finite direct sum of irreducible compact
representations of 0𝐺. Let us call these (𝜏𝑖, 𝐸𝑖). The restriction of 𝑉(𝜏) to 0𝐺is therefore a
direct sum of representations isomorphic to one of the (𝜏𝑖, 𝐸𝑖). Since 𝑉(𝜏) is generated by
𝑉(𝜏)𝐾, if 𝑉(𝜏) ≠{0}, necessarily one of the 𝐸𝑖contains a non-zero vector fixed by 𝐾. Since
𝐾⊂0𝐺, this then shows that 𝐸contains a non-zero vector fixed by 𝐾.
Now let (𝜌, 𝑊) be a non-zero supercuspidal subquotient of (𝜋, 𝑉). Then any irreducible
subquotient (𝜏, 𝐸) of (𝜌, 𝑊) is also an irreducible subquotient of (𝜋, 𝑉), and from what has
just been established, it contains a non-zero vector fixed by 𝐾. Since the functor 𝑉↦→𝑉𝐾is
exact (Proposition III.1.5), we deduce that (𝜌, 𝑊) contains a non-zero vector fixed by 𝐾.
□
Corollary. Let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, contained and normal in 𝐾0 and

𝑁maps (𝑉′)𝐾surjectively onto (𝑉′
𝑁)𝐾∩𝑀. It follows that
(𝑉′)𝐾is non-zero. It remains to show that (𝑉′)𝐾generates 𝑉′. Let 𝑊be the submodule of

Conversely, if (𝜋, 𝑉) is of finite length, an argument by induction on the length easily shows

length is finitely generated.
□

VI.7.4
Central idempotents
The decomposition theorem allows us to write the category M(𝐺) as a product of smaller
categories, parameterized by the connected components of the algebraic variety Ω(𝐺). For
any connected component Ω of Ω(𝐺), the projection
𝑒Ω : M(𝐺) →M(𝐺)Ω
defines an idempotent of the center of the category M(𝐺) and
IdM(𝐺) =
∑︁
Ω
𝑒Ω.
If the inertial support of Ω is 𝔰∈B(𝐺), we also denote 𝑒𝔰= 𝑒Ω.
The Hecke algebra H (𝐺) therefore decomposes into
H (𝐺) =
Ê
𝔰∈B(𝐺)
𝑒𝔰∗H (𝐺) ∗𝑒𝔰.
Each 𝑒𝔰∗H (𝐺) ∗𝑒𝔰is a two-sided ideal of H (𝐺). The category M(𝐺)𝔰is equivalent to the
category M(𝑒𝔰∗H (𝐺) ∗𝑒𝔰) of non-degenerate 𝑒𝔰∗H (𝐺) ∗𝑒𝔰-modules.
Remark. The idempotent 𝑒𝔰is not an element of the Hecke algebra H (𝐺), but of its completion
(cf. I.1.3) H (𝐺).
It remains to describe the center of each category M(𝐺)Ω. When we take a connected
component Ω of Ω(𝐺) given by an inertial class of supercuspidal representations of 𝐺, we
were able to describe the corresponding category as a category of unital modules over a unital
algebra, and describe the center of this category (i.e., the center of the unital algebra in
question), as the algebra of functions on Ω. We must obtain similar results for an arbitrary
connected component Ω. The first step consists of finding a progenerator for these categories.
Suppose that Ω is given by a Levi subgroup 𝑀and an inertial class 𝐷of supercuspidal
representations of 𝑀. The naive idea is to obtain this progenerator by inducing from 𝑀to
𝐺the progenerator of M(𝑀)𝐷. The difficulty that appears in this approach is to show that
we indeed obtain in this way a finitely generated projective object. The proof of this fact is
delicate, and uses the generalization of Jacquet’s lemma to non-admissible representations, as
well as the Noetherian properties of the category M(𝐺).

𝑃preserves finitely generated
representations. This involves Noetherian properties of the category M(𝐺) coming from the
decomposition theorem VI.7.2.
In an abelian category, an object is Noetherian if any increasing sequence of subobjects is
stationary (cf. Appendix A.I.2). An abelian category such that any finitely generated object
is Noetherian will be called Noetherian. For example, the category 𝐴−mod, where 𝐴is a
Noetherian ring, is Noetherian.
Proposition. The category M(𝐺) is Noetherian.
Proof. Let us first consider a supercuspidal component M(𝐺)𝔰of the decomposition VI.7.2, say
𝔰= [𝐺, (𝜌, 𝑊)]𝐺where (𝜌, 𝑊) is thus an irreducible supercuspidal representation of 𝐺. We saw
in Corollary VI.4.4 that the category M(𝐺)𝔰= M(𝐺)[𝜌] is equivalent to the category of right
unital modules over a certain Noetherian C-algebra. It is therefore a Noetherian category.
Let us return to the functor 𝑅: M(𝐺) →Cusp(𝐺) of Section VI.7.2. Since each category
M(𝑀)𝑠𝑐which constitutes Cusp(𝐺) decomposes (in the sense of A.IX) according to VI.3.5 into

true for their product: indeed, a finitely generated object has only a finite number of non-zero
components in Î
(𝑀,𝐷) M(𝑀)𝐷since by definition, this holds for a finite system of generators,
and the assertion easily follows. Suppose that (𝜋, 𝑉) is a finitely generated representation of

𝑉1 ⊂𝑉2 ⊂· · · ⊂𝑉𝑛⊂· · ·
be a sequence of subrepresentations of 𝑉. Since 𝑅is faithful and exact (VI.7.2), if this sequence

□

VI.8
Algebraic families of representations
VI.8.1
(𝐺, 𝐵)-modules
Let 𝐵be a reduced Noetherian finitely generated commutative C-algebra. We refer the reader
to [8] for the notion of a flat module.
Definition. A (𝐺, 𝐵)-module is a C-vector space 𝑉equipped with a smooth representation
(𝜋, 𝑉) of 𝐺and a flat 𝐵-module structure, where the two actions commute. Let M(𝐺, 𝐵)
denote the category of (𝐺, 𝐵)-modules. If 𝐾is a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, the space
𝑉𝐾is again a 𝐵-module. A (𝐺, 𝐵)-module 𝑉is admissible if for any compact open subgroup
𝐾of 𝐺, 𝑉𝐾is finitely generated over 𝐵.
Proposition. Let 𝑉be a C-vector space 𝑉equipped with a smooth representation (𝜋, 𝑉)
of 𝐺and a 𝐵-module structure, where the two actions commute. Then 𝑉is an admissible
(𝐺, 𝐵)-module if and only if for any compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺, 𝑉𝐾is a finitely generated
projective 𝐵-module.
Proof. According to Corollaire §1, n°6 and Proposition 5, §1, n°5 of [12], there is an equiv-
alence between finitely presented flat modules and finitely generated projective modules.
Since 𝐵is Noetherian, a finitely generated module is finitely presented ([12], Proposition 5
of A.X.4), so a finitely generated 𝐵-module is flat if and only if it is projective. On the other
hand, for any 𝐵-module E, the natural morphism
𝐸⊗𝐵𝑉𝐾→(𝐸⊗𝐵𝑉)𝐾
is an isomorphism whose inverse is given by
𝑒⊗𝑣↦→𝑒⊗𝑒𝐾· 𝑣.
Finally, the functor 𝑗𝐾: 𝑉↦→𝑉𝐾is exact, so if 𝑉is a flat 𝐵-module, i.e., if the functor • ⊗𝐵𝑉is
exact, then the functor
𝑗𝐾(• ⊗𝐵𝑉) ≃• ⊗𝐵𝑉𝐾
is exact, which implies that 𝑉𝐾is flat. This shows that if 𝑉is an admissible (𝐺, 𝐵)-module,
then 𝑉𝐾is a finitely generated projective module. Conversely, if the functor
• ⊗𝐵𝑉𝐾

is exact for any compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺, then for any short exact sequence of (𝐺, 𝐵)-

VI.8.5
Generic irreducibility of induced representations
Using the geometric lemma and Proposition VI.8.4 we establish the following theorem.
Theorem. Let 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁be a parabolic subgroup of 𝐺, and let (𝜌, 𝑊) be an irreducible
supercuspidal representation of 𝑀. For all 𝜓in X(𝑀), set 𝜋𝜓= 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜌⊗𝜓). Then 𝜋𝜓is
irreducible for all 𝜓in a non-empty Zariski open set of the algebraic variety X(𝑀).
Proof. By Proposition VI.8.4, it suffices to show that 𝜋𝜓0 is irreducible for a certain 𝜓0 in
X(𝑀).
Let 𝐴be the split component of 𝑀. Let 𝜒be the central character of 𝜌. The restriction of
the character |𝜒| of 𝑍(𝑀) to 𝐴takes values in R×
+, and thus is trivial on 0𝐴. It therefore defines
a character of Λ(𝐴) = 𝐴/0𝐴. Since this lattice injects with a finite index into Λ(𝑀) (cf. V.2.6),
we can extend |𝜒| to a character 𝜓∈X(𝑀). We obtain |𝜓−1𝜒| = 1 on 𝐴, so |𝜓−1𝜒| factors into a
character of the compact group 𝑍(𝑀)/𝐴and since any character of a compact group is unitary,
|𝜓−1𝜒| = 1 on 𝑍(𝑀). Since 𝜌⊗𝜓−1 is supercuspidal with unitary central character, it is unitary.
Indeed, a supercuspidal representation is compact modulo the center by Theorem VI.2.1, thus
essentially square-integrable modulo the center, and we can then conclude by Lemma IV.3.2.
By translating by such a character 𝜓−1, we may therefore assume, in the statement of the
theorem, that 𝜌is unitary. Moreover, for any unitary 𝜓∈X(𝑀), 𝜌⊗𝜓is then unitary, and
thus also 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜌⊗𝜓, 𝑊). In particular, this latter representation is completely reducible.

𝑃𝑖𝐺

VI.9.4
A consequence
A first consequence of the generalized Jacquet lemma is that Corollary VI.6.3 is now valid
without the admissibility hypothesis. Let us restate it:

Corollary. Let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, contained and normal in 𝐾0 and
admitting an Iwahori decomposition with respect to the standard parabolic subgroups. Let
(𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of 𝐺such that 𝑉𝐾generates 𝑉. Then any subquotient of 𝑉
is generated by its vectors fixed by 𝐾.
We deduce from this, by virtue of Theorem I.3.2:
Proposition. Let 𝐾be a compact open subgroup of 𝐺, contained and normal in 𝐾0 and
admitting an Iwahori decomposition with respect to the standard parabolic subgroups. Then
the full subcategory of M(𝐺) of representations generated by their 𝐾-invariant vectors is
equivalent to the category of unital modules over the Hecke algebra H (𝐺, 𝐾).

as a ℨ(𝐺)-module.

VI.10.8
Return to Howe’s theorem
Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of 𝐺. We say that (𝜋, 𝑉) is ℨ(𝐺)-finite if the annihilator
of (𝜋, 𝑉) in ℨ(𝐺) is an ideal of finite codimension.
We conclude our study of the relations between admissibility, finite generation and the
action of ℨ(𝐺) with the following theorem.
Theorem. Let (𝜋, 𝑉) be a smooth representation of 𝐺and consider the following possible
properties of (𝜋, 𝑉):
(1) (𝜋, 𝑉) is finitely generated,
(2) (𝜋, 𝑉) is admissible,
(3) (𝜋, 𝑉) is ℨ(𝐺)-finite.
Then any two of these properties imply the third, and the representation (𝜋, 𝑉) is then
of finite length.
Proof. Howe’s theorem VI.6.3 asserts that (1) and (2) are equivalent to (𝜋, 𝑉) being of finite
length. It is easy to see by induction on the length that a representation of finite length is
ℨ(𝐺)-finite, starting with the case where (𝜋, 𝑉) is irreducible (this is then Schur’s lemma).
Suppose that (𝜋, 𝑉) satisfies (1) and (3). We saw in VI.10.7 that (1) implies that (𝜋, 𝑉) is
ℨ(𝐺)-admissible, i.e., that for any compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺, 𝑉𝐾is a finitely generated
ℨ(𝐺)-module. But (3) tells us that 𝑉𝐾is annihilated by an ideal of finite codimension of ℨ(𝐺),
thus 𝑉𝐾is finite-dimensional. This shows that (𝜋, 𝑉) is admissible.
Suppose that (𝜋, 𝑉) satisfies (2) and (3). By the characterization of finitely generated
representations of Proposition VI.10.7, we must show that (𝜋, 𝑉) is ℨ(𝐺)-admissible, and has
only a finite number of non-trivial Bernstein components 𝑉𝔰. The first point is clear, since
for any compact open subgroup 𝐾of 𝐺, 𝑉𝐾is finite-dimensional, thus finitely generated over
ℨ(𝐺). The second is as well, since an ideal of finite codimension of ℨ(𝐺) will contain the
components ℨ𝔰except for a finite number of 𝔰∈B(𝐺), and on the other hand, the identity of
ℨ𝔰acts by the identity on 𝑉𝔰.
□


## Page 217


VI.11. Notes on Chapter VI
211
VI.11
Notes on Chapter VI
Generalities on induction and restriction functors can be found in many places in the literature.
The same is true for the main results on supercuspidal representations ([4], [5], [20]). The
attribution of results is sometimes delicate. Thus, the various characterizations of supercuspidal
representations (Theorem VI.2.1) seem to be due to H. Jacquet for 𝐺= GL(𝑛, F) and to Harish-
Chandra in general, and the name Harish-Chandra’s theorem seems to have prevailed (perhaps
also because other important results bear Jacquet’s name). I also used [23] for the writing of
the proof of this theorem. The admissibility and uniform admissibility theorems, as well as
Proposition VI.2.4 are due to Bernstein ([1], [4]). Here again, the notes of S. DeBacker [23]
were useful to me. The study of the category M(𝐺)[𝜋] and the determination of its center is
based on the notes [38]. The geometric lemma is due independently to Bernstein-Zelevinskii
[5] and Casselman [20], the proof follows [5] for a good part, but also [20] at a crucial point,
which allows for an explicit integral formula (VI.5.1.12). The consequences of the geometric
lemma on the composition series of parabolic inductions of supercuspidal representations
VI.5.3 and VI.5.4 are obtained in [5]. The generic irreducibility of induced representations
VI.8.5 is taken from [2]. The proof of Jacquet’s lemma VI.6.1 written here borrows from
several sources, in particular [4], [2], [23]. Howe’s theorem is taken from [4]. The proof of
the Bernstein decomposition theorem essentially follows the notes [38], notes themselves
inspired by [2] to which I referred from time to time. The second adjunction theorem is
established, for the main lines of the proof, in [2] (see also [3] and [22]). I tried to make it
more accessible by adding some details. It nevertheless remains a difficult and subtle proof,
the crucial point of which is the stabilization lemma C.II used in VI.8.1. The second adjunction
theorem is equivalent to a strong form of Casselman’s duality, valid without the admissibility
hypothesis ([2] and [3]).
The results on the Bernstein center are presented here following the ideas of [2] and [3],
taken up and detailed in the notes [38]. The approach, as we have explained, is somewhat
different from the one followed in the earlier draft [24], but the important corollaries of
the main result are taken from [24]. The statement of Theorem VI.10.8 is inspired by the
analogous result for real groups, which can be found scattered in [35].


## Page 219


CHAPTER VII
Langlands Classification
The goal of this chapter is to state and prove the Langlands classification theorem, which
reduces the problem of determining Irr(𝐺), i.e., the isomorphism classes of irreducible smooth
representations of a 𝑝-adic reductive group 𝐺, to the problem of determining the classes of
irreducible tempered representations. The definition of a tempered representation given here
relies solely on normalized exponents, defined in the first section. Casselman’s criterion pro-
vides a necessary and sufficient condition on the normalized exponents of a representation for
it to be square-integrable modulo the center: they must lie in certain open cones. A tempered
representation is then defined as one whose normalized exponents all lie in the closures of
these open cones. A tempered representation is therefore close to being a square-integrable
modulo the center representation, and moreover, we show that an irreducible tempered
representation appears as a subrepresentation of a parabolic induction of a representation
square-integrable modulo the center. In particular, since it embeds into the parabolic induction
of a unitary representation, it is unitary. We show on the other hand that parabolic induction
preserves the class of tempered representations. This is not the case for parabolic restriction,
but one can define a notion of tempered parabolic restriction, which preserves the class of
tempered representations, and even obtain a tempered version of Frobenius reciprocity and
the geometric lemma.
We then study the intertwining operators between representations of the form 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜌, 𝑊)
and 𝑖𝐺
𝑄(𝜌, 𝑊), where 𝑃and 𝑄are two parabolic subgroups with the same Levi factor 𝑀
and (𝜌, 𝑊) is a smooth representation of 𝑀. When (𝜌, 𝑊) satisfies a certain criterion (𝑃𝑄-
regularity), we exhibit a canonical intertwining operator between these representations. The
transitivity properties of these intertwining operators are studied in detail. A Langlands triplet
consists of a parabolic subgroup 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁of 𝐺, an irreducible tempered representation (𝜎, 𝐸)
of 𝑀and an unramified character 𝜓of 𝑀satisfying a certain positivity condition. We then
show that the representation 𝜎⊗𝜓is 𝑃¯𝑃-regular, and thus that there exists an intertwining
operator between 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜎, 𝐸) and 𝑖𝐺
¯𝑃(𝜎, 𝐸). A more refined study shows that the space of such
operators is 1-dimensional, and that 𝑖𝐺
𝑃(𝜎, 𝐸) (resp. 𝑖𝐺
¯𝑃(𝜎, 𝐸)) admits a unique irreducible
quotient (resp. subrepresentation). We call this quotient the Langlands quotient of the
triplet. The classification theorem asserts that any irreducible representation of 𝐺appears as
a Langlands quotient, in an essentially unique way.

An application

VII.2
Tempered representations
In this section, we develop the algebraic theory of tempered representations. For the analytic
aspects (asymptotic behavior of the character and matrix coefficients), we refer the reader to
[45].
VII.2.1
Definition by normalized exponents
Definition. A representation (𝜋, 𝑉) of M(𝐺) is said to be tempered if it is admissible,
and if for any parabolic subgroup 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁, any normalized exponent 𝜒of 𝜋with respect
to 𝑃satisfies ℜ(𝜒) ∈+[𝔞∗

VII.2.6
Irreducible tempered representations
The following result shows that tempered representations are obtained as subrepresentations
of induced representations of discrete series.

and only if there exists a standard parabolic subgroup 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁of 𝐺and an irreducible square-
integrable representation modulo the center (𝜎, 𝐸) of 𝑀such that (𝜋, 𝑉) is a subrepresentation
of 𝑖𝐺

Corollary. An irreducible tempered representation is unitary.
Proof. This follows from the theorem because an irreducible square-integrable representation
is unitary (IV.3.2) and parabolic induction preserves unitarity.
□
Remark. Without the irreducibility hypothesis in the preceding corollary, the conclusion of the
latter can be false.

- a standard parabolic subgroup 𝑃= 𝑀𝑁,
- an irreducible tempered representation (𝜎, 𝐸) of 𝑀,
- an unramified character 𝜓∈X(𝑀) such that ℜ(𝜓) ∈𝐺

## Page 245


VII.5. Notes on Chapter VII
239
VII.5
Notes on Chapter VII
The proof of Casselman’s criterion written here borrows from [2], [20] and especially [23].
The application VII.1.3 is taken from [2]; recall that this is a crucial point to show that the
small progenerators of the components of the Bernstein decomposition exhibited in VI.10.1
are indeed generators. The section on tempered representations is taken from [45], the one on
intertwining operators from [22]. The exposition of the Langlands classification also follows
that of [22]. The reader can also refer to [40] for another exposition.


## Page 247


APPENDIX A
Elements of category theory
A.I
Categories and functors
We assume that the reader possesses some rudiments of category theory, i.e., at least the
basic definitions and terminology (object, morphisms, functors, subcategory...), as can be
found in any good book on the subject, for example [27] or [37]. For a concise and rigorous
introduction (in French) to this subject, covering all the aspects we need in this book, we
refer to Chapter II of [25]. In addition to general statements which, for the convenience of
the reader, we recall - and sometimes prove -, we develop a certain number of heterogeneous
examples, because they appeared in the main body of the book. We will not delve into the
logical foundations of category theory, which we use from a naive point of view, hoping that
everything we say can be made rigorous by standard procedures (Grothendieck universes...).
Let 𝒞be a category. We simply write 𝑋∈𝒞to say that 𝑋is an object of 𝒞and 𝑓: 𝑋→𝑌
(or 𝑋
𝑓→𝑌), to say that 𝑓is a morphism from 𝑋to 𝑌. We denote by Hom𝒞(𝑋, 𝑌) the set of
morphisms from 𝑋to 𝑌and Id𝑋the identity of 𝑋.
The notation for functors are standard. If 𝐹is a functor from the category A to the category
B, if 𝑋, 𝑌are objects of A and if 𝑓is a morphism from 𝑋to 𝑌, we denote respectively by
𝐹(𝑋), 𝐹(𝑌) and 𝐹( 𝑓) their images under the functor 𝐹. Often, in the mathematical literature,
functors are explicitly defined only on objects. Their effect on morphisms is generally obvious,
and it is part of the reader’s task to make it explicit.
A.I.1

We begin by introducing some definitions concerning morphisms. A morphism 𝑋
𝑓→𝑌is
an isomorphism, if there exists a morphism 𝑌
𝑔→𝑋such that 𝑔𝑓= Id𝑋and 𝑓𝑔= Id𝑌. It is a
monomorphism if the only pairs of morphisms 𝑍

An isomorphism is both an epimorphism and a monomorphism, but the converse is false
in general. It is true in abelian categories.

Appendix A. Elements of category theory
A.I.2
Subobjects, quotient objects
Let us now introduce the notions of subobjects and quotient objects of an object 𝑋in a category
𝒞. Consider the preorder ≤on the monomorphisms 𝑌
𝑓→𝑋:
𝑌1

if there exists a morphism 𝑌1
𝑖→𝑌2 such that

Note then that 𝑖is a monomorphism. We say that 𝑓1 and 𝑓2 are equivalent if 𝑓1 ≤𝑓2 and
𝑓2 ≤𝑓1. A subobject of 𝑋is an equivalence class of monomorphisms into 𝑋for this equivalence
relation. We denote by 𝑆𝑋the collection of subobjects of 𝑋. It is clear that ≤induces an
order relation on 𝑆𝑋. Dually, we define the quotient objects of 𝑋: we equip the collection of
epimorphisms 𝑋
𝑓→𝑌with the preorder ≤given by
𝑋
𝑓1→𝑌1 ≤𝑋

if there exists a morphism 𝑌2 →𝑌1 such that
𝑋

We say that 𝑓1 and 𝑓2 are equivalent if 𝑓1 ≤𝑓2 and 𝑓2 ≤𝑓1. A quotient of 𝑋is an equivalence
class of epimorphisms and ≤induces an order on the collection 𝑄𝑋of quotients of 𝑋.
If 𝐶and 𝐷are two subobjects of 𝑋, we call the intersection of 𝐶and 𝐷, and we denote by
𝐶∩𝐷, a greatest lower bound of 𝐶and 𝐷in 𝑆𝑋. Similarly, we call the union of 𝐶and 𝐷, and
we denote by 𝐶∪𝐷, a least upper bound of 𝐶and 𝐷in 𝑆𝑋. Of course, such objects do not
necessarily exist but if they exist, they are unique. We can also obviously extend these notions
by defining the intersection and the union of an arbitrary family of subobjects of 𝑋. We will
say that the category 𝒞admits arbitrary (resp. finite) intersections (resp. unions), if for any
object 𝑋of 𝒞and any family (resp. finite family) of subobjects of 𝑋, the intersection (resp.
the union) of this family exists.
Very often, instead of saying: (the class of) 𝑌
𝑓→𝑋is a subobject of 𝑋, we just say: 𝑌is a
subobject of 𝑋, and we write 𝑌⊆𝑋.
A.I.3
Noetherian and finitely generated objects
Let 𝑋be an object of 𝒞. Let 𝑈be a subset of 𝑆𝑋and 𝐴a subobject of 𝑋in 𝑈. We say that 𝐴is
maximal in 𝑈, if for any subobject 𝐵of 𝑋in 𝑈such that 𝐴⊆𝐵, we have 𝐵= 𝐴. We say that 𝑋
is Noetherian if for any non-empty subset 𝑈of 𝑆𝑋, there exists a maximal element in 𝑈. We
can also define the notion of a Noetherian object by a maximal chain condition. A chain in 𝑆𝑋
is a totally ordered subset of 𝑆𝑋. It is then easy to verify that the object 𝑋is Noetherian if and
only if any chain in 𝑆𝑋contains a maximal element ([25], Proposition 1.6.1). In practice, it
suffices to consider well-ordered chains, i.e., increasing sequences of subobjects.
Another very closely related notion is that of a finitely generated object in a category.
We assume here that the category 𝒞admits arbitrary unions. We say that an object 𝑋of
𝒞is finitely generated if for any chain of proper subobjects (𝐴𝑖) of 𝑋, Ð
𝑖𝐴𝑖is still a proper
subobject of 𝑋(proper means not isomorphic to 𝑋). We can characterize finitely generated
objects by a compactness property. The object 𝑋of 𝒞is finitely generated if for any family of
subobjects (𝐴𝑖) of 𝑋such that Ð
𝑖𝐴𝑖= 𝑋, there exists a finite number of subobjects 𝐴𝑖1, . . . , 𝐴𝑖𝑛
such that Ð𝑛
𝑗=1 𝐴𝑖𝑗= 𝑋(see [37] 4.10, Theorem 1).
It is clear from the definitions that a subobject of a Noetherian object is finitely generated.
Indeed, consider a chain of proper subobjects (𝐴𝑖)𝑖of a subobject 𝑌of a Noetherian object

A.I. Categories and functors

𝑋. Then by the Noetherian property, this chain admits a maximal element, say 𝐵, and it is
clear that this maximal object is a least upper bound of the family (𝐴𝑖)𝑖, i.e., 𝐵= Ð
𝑖𝐴𝑖⊂𝑌.
It follows that 𝐵= Ð
𝑖𝐴𝑖is a proper subobject of 𝑌, which shows that 𝑌is finitely generated.
Conversely, if every subobject of 𝑋is finitely generated, then 𝑋is Noetherian. Indeed, let 𝐵𝑖
be an increasing sequence of subobjects and set 𝐵= Ð
𝑖𝐵𝑖. Since 𝐵is finitely generated, there

A category where every finitely generated object is Noetherian will be called Noetherian.
Example. A module 𝑀over a unital ring 𝐴is a finitely generated object in the category M(𝐴)
if and only if 𝑀is finitely generated over 𝐴in the algebraic sense ([25], Lemma 3.6.1). The
category M(𝐴) is Noetherian if and only if the ring 𝐴is Noetherian.
A.I.4
Initial, final, zero object
Let us now define the notions of initial, final or zero object. An object 𝑋in a category 𝒞is
said to be initial (resp. final) if for any object 𝑌of 𝒞, Hom𝒞(𝑋, 𝑌) (resp. Hom𝒞(𝑌, 𝑋)) is a
singleton. A zero object is an object that is both initial and final. Initial, final, or zero objects
are unique up to isomorphism. If 𝒞admits a zero object 0𝒞, all the sets Hom𝒞(𝑋, 𝑌) possess
a distinguished object, namely the composition
𝑋→0𝒞→𝑌
which we will simply denote by 0. Under these conditions, we can define the kernel of a

A.I.5
Product category, opposite category
Let A and B be two categories. We form the category A ×B whose objects are the pairs (𝑋, 𝑌),
𝑋∈A, 𝑌∈B, and the morphisms between (𝑋, 𝑌) and (𝑋′, 𝑌′) are the pairs of morphisms
( 𝑓, 𝑔) ∈HomA(𝑋, 𝑋′) × HomB(𝑌, 𝑌′). We also form the category A𝑜𝑝whose objects are
the same as A, and for any pair (𝑋, 𝑋′) of objects, HomA𝑜𝑝(𝑋, 𝑋′) = HomA(𝑋′, 𝑋). The use


## Page 250


244
Appendix A. Elements of category theory
of the opposite category allows us to dispense with the notion of a contravariant functor;
consequently, all functors considered will always be covariant unless explicitly stated otherwise.
Another conceptual contribution of the introduction of opposite categories is the notion of
duality. As we have seen in the various definitions given above, these often come in pairs, one
being obtained from the other "by reversing the direction of the arrows", i.e., by working in
the opposite categories. Thus, the pairs monomorphism/epimorphism, subobject/quotient
object, initial object/final object, kernel/cokernel are dual. We will see many other instances
of this principle in what follows. In practice, any statement in category theory admits a dual
statement, whose proof is obtained by reversing the direction of the arrows.
A.II
Natural transformation, equivalence of categories
A.II.1
Natural transformations
Let A and B be two categories, and 𝐹, 𝐺two functors from A to B. A natural transformation
𝜃from 𝐹to 𝐺is the data for any object 𝑋of A of a morphism
𝜃𝑋: 𝐹(𝑋) →𝐺(𝑋)
such that for any morphism 𝑓: 𝑋→𝑌in A, the diagram
𝐹(𝑋)
𝐹( 𝑓)
−−−−−−→𝐹(𝑌)
y𝜃𝑋
y𝜃𝑌
𝐺(𝑋)
𝐺( 𝑓)
−−−−−−→𝐺(𝑌)
commutes.
For any pair of categories (A, B), we can form a category FA,B whose objects are the
functors from A to B, and the morphisms are the natural transformations between these
functors. We therefore denote by Hom(𝐹, 𝐺) the set of natural transformations from 𝐹to 𝐺
and Id𝐹the trivial natural transformation from the functor 𝐹to itself. A natural isomorphism
between 𝐹and 𝐺is a natural transformation 𝜃∈Hom(𝐹, 𝐺) admitting an inverse, i.e., a
natural transformation 𝜙∈Hom(𝐺, 𝐹) such that 𝜃𝜙= Id𝐺, 𝜙𝜃= Id𝐹.
Any category A is equipped with an identity functor, which we denote by I𝑑A:
I𝑑A : A →A,
𝑋↦→𝑋,
𝑓↦→𝑓,
for any object 𝑋and any morphism 𝑓of A.
A.II.2
Equivalences of categories
One of the important ideas at the origin of category theory is that, in many cases, one is
more interested in the isomorphism classes of objects of a given category than in the objects
themselves. This is particularly true in representation theory for example, where the problems
of classifying irreducible representations are always "up to isomorphism". Heuristically, if we
start from a category 𝒞, imagine that we form the subcategory ¯𝒞whose objects are given by
a choice of representatives of the isomorphism classes of objects of 𝒞, the morphisms between
two representatives being all the morphisms between these objects in 𝒞(in other words, it
is a full subcategory). We would like a definition of equivalence of categories that makes 𝒞
and ¯𝒞equivalent. Requiring the existence of functors 𝐹: 𝒞→¯𝒞and 𝐺: ¯𝒞→𝒞inverse to
each other, i.e., such that 𝐹𝐺= I𝑑¯𝒞, 𝐺𝐹= I𝑑𝒞as a definition of equivalence is too strong a
constraint to obtain this. The adequate definition is


## Page 251


A.III. Universal problems and representable functors
245
Definition. Let 𝐹: A →B be a functor between two categories A and B. We say that
𝐹is an equivalence of categories if there exists a functor 𝐺: B →A such that 𝐹𝐺is
naturally isomorphic to I𝑑A, and 𝐺𝐹naturally isomorphic to I𝑑B. The categories A and
B are then said to be equivalent and the functors 𝐹and 𝐺are said to be quasi-inverses.
Example. Let Vectn
k be the category of vector spaces of dimension 𝑛over the field 𝑘whose
morphisms are linear maps. Let 𝑉𝑛
𝑘be the category whose unique object is the vector space 𝑘𝑛
and whose morphisms are the endomorphisms of 𝑘𝑛. As functor 𝐹, we take the inclusion of 𝑉𝑛
𝑘
into Vectn
k. It is an equivalence of categories.
This example well illustrates the idea expressed above, since all the objects of Vectn
k are
isomorphic to 𝑘𝑛. Note that the construction of a quasi-inverse 𝐺requires the choice of such
an isomorphism for any object (in other words the choice of a basis). There is therefore no
uniqueness of the quasi-inverse, and its construction uses the axiom of choice.
The functor 𝐹: A →B is said to be full (resp. faithful) if for any pair of objects (𝑋, 𝑌) in
A, 𝐹realizes a surjection (resp. an injection) between HomA(𝑋, 𝑌) and HomB(𝐹(𝑋), 𝐹(𝑌)).
A full and faithful functor is said to be fully faithful. A subcategory A of a category B is said
to be full if the inclusion functor A →B is fully faithful.
We often use the following criterion to show that a functor is an equivalence of categories
([28], Theorem 1.13 or [25], Theorem 2.3.5).
Theorem. A functor 𝐹: A →B is an equivalence of categories if it is fully faithful and if
any object of B is isomorphic to an object of the form 𝐹(𝑋), where 𝑋∈A.
A.III
Universal problems and representable functors
We now introduce the notion of a universal problem and of a solution to such a problem.
Certain examples chosen to illustrate this notion have appeared in the text, such as for example
the localization of a vector space with respect to an endomorphism. We take this opportunity
to establish a lemma and its corollary, used in VI.9.1.
A.III.1
Universal problems
Definition. Let 𝐹: A →B be a functor and let 𝑌be an object of B. We say that the
object 𝑋of A and the morphism 𝑖: 𝑌→𝐹(𝑋) are solutions of the right universal problem
posed by 𝐹and 𝑌if for any object 𝑍of A and any morphism 𝑓: 𝑌→𝐹(𝑍), there exists
a unique morphism 𝑔: 𝑋→𝑍satisfying 𝐹(𝑔) ◦𝑖= 𝑓. Similarly we say that 𝑋∈A and
𝑗: 𝐹(𝑋) →𝑌are solutions of the left universal problem posed by 𝐹and 𝑌if for any object
𝑍of A and any morphism 𝑓: 𝐹(𝑍) →𝑌, there exists a unique morphism 𝑔: 𝑍→𝑋
satisfying 𝑗◦𝐹(𝑔) = 𝑓.
Remark. If two pairs (𝑋, 𝑖) and (𝑋′, 𝑖′) are solutions of the (right) universal problem posed
by 𝐹and 𝑌, then 𝑋and 𝑋′ are isomorphic, up to a unique isomorphism, as can be seen by
taking as morphism 𝑓successively the morphisms 𝑖and 𝑖′. We indeed obtain two morphisms
𝑔1 : 𝑋→𝑋′ and 𝑔2 : 𝑋′ →𝑋satisfying 𝐹(𝑔1) ◦𝑖= 𝑖′ and 𝐹(𝑔2) ◦𝑖′ = 𝑖, whence 𝐹(𝑔2 ◦𝑔1) ◦𝑖= 𝑖
and 𝐹(𝑔1 ◦𝑔2) ◦𝑖′ = 𝑖′. Since the morphisms Id𝑋and Id𝑋′ also satisfy 𝐹(Id𝑋) ◦𝑖= 𝑖and
𝐹(Id𝑋′) ◦𝑖′ = 𝑖′, by uniqueness, 𝑔2 ◦𝑔1 = Id𝑋and 𝑔1 ◦𝑔2 = Id𝑋′. The same holds for left
universal problems.
Examples. 1. Coproducts, products. Let A be a category. Consider the "diagonal" functor
Δ : A →A × A,
𝑋↦→(𝑋, 𝑋),
𝑓↦→( 𝑓, 𝑓)

and let us be given an element (𝑋, 𝑌) of A × A. A solution of the left universal problem posed
by Δ and (𝑋, 𝑌) is called the product of 𝑋and 𝑌and denoted 𝑋× 𝑌. Similarly the coproduct
(or "direct sum"), denoted 𝑋⨿𝑌(or 𝑋⊕𝑌), is the solution of the right universal problem posed
by Δ and (𝑋, 𝑌).
— 2. Completion of a topological space.
Let EvTop and EvTop be the categories of
topological vector spaces and complete topological vector spaces respectively, the morphisms
being continuous maps. The functor we consider is the (fully faithful) inclusion of EvTop
into EvTop. We are given 𝑌∈EvTop. A solution of the right universal problem posed by the
inclusion and 𝑌is the completion of 𝑌, and the continuous inclusion 𝑌↩→¯𝑌. More generally,
one can define the completion of a topological space admitting a uniform structure, the
uniform structure being necessary to define the notion of a Cauchy filter (see [10]).
— 3. Localization of a ring. Let A be the category whose objects are pairs (𝐴, 𝑆) consisting
of a commutative unital ring 𝐴and a multiplicative subset 𝑆of 𝐴, the morphisms between
two objects (𝐴, 𝑆) and (𝐴′, 𝑆′) being unital ring morphisms 𝜙: 𝐴→𝐴′ such that 𝜙(𝑆) ⊂𝑆′.
We consider the full subcategory A′ of A whose objects are pairs (𝐴, 𝑆), with 𝑆contained in
the invertible elements of 𝐴. The solution of the right universal problem posed by an element
(𝐴, 𝑆) of A and the inclusion of A′ into A is the localization 𝑆−1𝐴of the ring 𝐴at 𝑆and the
natural morphism 𝐴→𝑆−1𝐴. Note that if 0 ∈𝑆, we have 𝑆−1𝐴= {0}.
— 4. Localization of a module. Let 𝐴be a commutative unital ring, and 𝑆a multiplicative
subset of 𝐴. Consider the full subcategory M(𝐴)𝑆of M(𝐴) whose objects are modules 𝑀such
that the action of any element 𝑠∈𝑆on 𝑀is invertible (i.e., for all 𝑠∈𝑆, for all 𝑚∈𝑀, there
exists 𝑡∈𝐴such that 𝑡𝑠· 𝑚= 𝑠𝑡· 𝑚= 𝑚). The right universal problem posed by a module 𝑀of
M(𝐴) and by the forgetful functor from M(𝐴)𝑆to M(𝐴) admits as a solution the localization
𝑆−1𝑀and the natural morphism 𝑀→𝑆−1𝑀(viewed simply as an 𝐴-module, and not as an
𝑆−1𝐴-module). Here again, if 0 ∈𝑆, we have 𝑆−1𝑀= {0}.
— 5. Localization of a vector space with respect to an endomorphism.
Let 𝑘be a
field, 𝑘[𝑋] the ring of polynomials with coefficients in 𝑘, and M(𝑘[𝑋]) the category of left
unital 𝑘[𝑋]-modules. A 𝑘[𝑋]-module can be viewed as the data of a vector space over 𝑘
and an endomorphism of this vector space. The category M(𝑘[𝑋]) is therefore equivalent
to the one whose objects are pairs (𝐿, 𝑎) where 𝐿is a vector space over 𝑘and 𝑎∈End𝑘(𝐿),
the morphisms between two objects (𝐿, 𝑎) and (𝐿′, 𝑎′) being linear maps between 𝐿and 𝐿′
intertwining 𝑎and 𝑎′. Consider in 𝑘[𝑋] the multiplicative subset {𝑋𝑘}𝑘∈N of powers of 𝑋.
The previous example asserts the existence, for any 𝑘[𝑋]-module 𝐿, of a localization with
respect to the subset {𝑋𝑘}𝑘∈N. Let us translate this in terms of vector spaces equipped with
endomorphisms by the equivalence of categories described above: given a pair (𝐿, 𝑎) where
𝐿is a vector space over 𝑘and 𝑎∈End𝑘(𝐿), there exists a pair (𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎), where 𝐿𝑎is a vector
space over 𝑘and ˜𝑎∈End𝑘(𝐿𝑎) is invertible, and a morphism 𝜄: (𝐿, 𝑎) →(𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎) such that
for any morphism 𝑓: (𝐿, 𝑎) →(𝑀, 𝑏), where 𝑏∈End𝑘(𝑀) is invertible, there exists a unique
morphism ˜𝑓: (𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎) →(𝑀, 𝑏) such that 𝑓= ˜𝑓◦𝜄. We call (𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎) the localization of 𝐿at 𝑎. If 𝐵

functor 𝐹.

unique isomorphism.
A.III.3
An isomorphism principle
We now reformulate Yoneda’s lemma in a form useful for showing that two objects of a
category are isomorphic, or that two functors are naturally isomorphic.
Proposition. Let 𝑋, 𝑌be two objects of a category M such that for any object 𝑍of M, we
have an isomorphism, natural in 𝑍:
(A.III.3.1)
Hom𝒞(𝑍, 𝑋) ≃Hom𝒞(𝑍, 𝑌).
Then 𝑋≃𝑌. If 𝐹, 𝐺: 𝒞→M are two functors from a category 𝒞to M such that we have
a natural isomorphism in 𝑋and 𝑍:
(A.III.3.2)
Hom𝒞(𝑍, 𝐹(𝑋)) ≃Hom𝒞(𝑍, 𝐺(𝑋)).
Then the functors 𝐹and 𝐺are naturally isomorphic.

A.IV.2
Colimits
We define dually the notion of colimit of a functor 𝐷: I →𝒞as in the previous paragraph: it
consists of a constant functor Δ𝑋: I →𝒞and a natural transformation from 𝐷to Δ𝑋, such
that for any constant functor Δ𝑌: I →𝒞and any natural transformation from 𝐷to Δ𝑌, there
exists a unique natural transformation Δ𝑋→Δ𝑌(i.e., a unique morphism 𝑋→𝑌) such that
𝐷→Δ𝑋→Δ𝑌= 𝐷→Δ𝑌.
A colimit is a solution of the right universal problem posed by Δ and 𝐷.
As examples of colimits, we have the dual notions of those of the examples of the previous
paragraph. We thus obtain respectively initial objects, cokernels, coproducts, pushouts,
inductive (or direct) limits, unions of subobjects. The notation used for colimits is lim
−−→.
Let us make the example of inductive limits a little more explicit. Let 𝐼be an upward
directed poset. We consider I as a category, where the morphisms, besides the identities of
the objects, are given by the pairs (𝑖, 𝑗) with 𝑖≤𝑗, the corresponding morphism being 𝑖→𝑗.
Let 𝐷: I →𝒞be a functor, and set, for all 𝑖, 𝑗in I, 𝐴𝑖= 𝐷(𝑖), 𝐷(𝑖→𝑗) = 𝑓𝑖𝑗. We say that
((𝐴𝑖)𝑖∈𝐼, ( 𝑓𝑖𝑗)𝑖≤𝑗) is a direct system . Then the inductive limit (or direct limit) of the system of
(𝐴𝑖, 𝑓𝑖𝑗) is the colimit of 𝐷.

A.V
Adjoint functors
There are two (equivalent) ways of viewing the notion of adjoint functors. Depending on the
application, one perspective may be more suitable than the other.

The following theorem links the notions of adjoint functors and representable functors.
Theorem. Let 𝐺: B →A be a functor. Then 𝐺admits a right adjoint if and only if for any
object 𝑋of A, the contravariant functor
𝑌↦→HomA(𝐺(𝑌), 𝑋)
from B to Ens is representable.
Proof. The condition is obviously necessary, since if 𝐺admits 𝐹as a right adjoint, the functor
𝑌↦→HomA(𝐺(𝑌), 𝑋) is represented by (𝐹(𝑋), 𝜃). We will now show that it is sufficient. Recall
that by Yoneda’s lemma, the functor
ℎ: B →FB𝑜𝑝,Ens
is fully faithful. It therefore realizes an equivalence of categories onto the full subcategory of
representable functors of FB𝑜𝑝,Ens. Let Λ be a quasi-inverse of ℎand let 𝐻: A →FB𝑜𝑝,Ens be
the functor defined by 𝐻(𝑋)(𝑌) = Hom(𝐺(𝑌), 𝑋), which by hypothesis takes values in the full
subcategory of representable functors of FB𝑜𝑝,Ens. Then 𝐹= Λ ◦𝐻is the desired adjoint.
□
Corollary. There is uniqueness up to isomorphism of the right adjoint of a functor 𝐺: B →A.
The same is true for left adjoints.

and (A.V.2.2).
The following result is used very often.
Theorem. Let 𝐹: A →B, 𝐺: B →A be two functors, 𝐺being the left adjoint of 𝐹. Then
𝐹preserves limits and 𝐺colimits.

←−−𝐷). We use Proposition A.III.3. For all 𝑌∈B, we have
HomB(𝑌, 𝐹(lim
←−−𝐷)) = HomA(𝐺(𝑌), lim
←−−𝐷) ≃HomFI,A (Δ𝐺(𝑌), 𝐷)
≃HomFI,B (Δ𝑌, 𝐹(𝐷)) = HomB(𝑌, lim

We have successively used the adjunction of 𝐹and 𝐺, the natural isomorphism (A.IV.1.1),

A.VI
Abelian categories
In this section, we recall the fundamental results concerning abelian categories. Our references
on the subject will be the books [27] and [37], to which the reader is invited to refer for
further details.


## Page 261


A.VI. Abelian categories
255
A.VI.1
Axiomatics of abelian categories
The notion of an abelian category axiomatizes the fundamental properties of the category
𝐴−mod of left modules over a ring 𝐴. Any full subcategory of a category 𝐴−mod stable
under passing to submodules and quotients is again an abelian category, for example:
- the category M(𝐴) of left unital modules over a unital ring 𝐴,
- the category of sheaves (or presheaves) of abelian groups on a topological space.
Let us now give the axioms defining abelian categories. Those given here are not the
most economical. Let A be a category. We say that A is an abelian category if it satisfies the
following axioms.
AB0. There exists a zero object in A, denoted 0.
AB1. The category A admits finite products.
AB1∗. The category A admits finite coproducts.
AB2. Every morphism in A admits a kernel.
AB2∗. Every morphism in A admits a cokernel.
AB3. Every monomorphism in A is a kernel.
AB3∗. Every epimorphism in A is a cokernel.
The custom in abelian categories is to use the terminology "direct sum" and the notation
É rather than "coproduct" and Ý.
A.VI.2
Properties of abelian categories
We deduce many consequences from these axioms. Here are some of the most important ones.
— 1. A morphism in A is an isomorphism if and only if it is both a monomorphism and
an epimorphism.
— 2. Let 𝐴be an object of A, and let 𝑆𝐴be the set of its subobjects, 𝑄𝐴the set of its
quotients. Then the maps
coker: 𝑆𝐴→𝑄𝐴,
ker: 𝑄𝐴→𝑆𝐴
which, respectively, associate to any subobject 𝐵→𝐴its cokernel, and to any quotient 𝐴→𝐶
its kernel, are inverse to each other. Moreover, they reverse the order on 𝑆𝐴and 𝑄𝐴. We
denote by 𝐴/𝐵the quotient coker(𝐵→𝐴) for any subobject 𝐵→𝐴of 𝐴.
— 3. Any two subobjects of an object 𝐴admit an intersection and a union. As for coproducts,
it is often more expressive to change the terminology for unions in abelian categories by
speaking instead of sums. We then use the notation + or Í.
We define the image of a morphism 𝑓: 𝐴→𝐵in A as the smallest subobject 𝐶→𝐵of 𝐵
such that 𝑓factors through 𝐶→𝐵. Dually, we define the coimage of 𝑓as the smallest quotient
𝐴→𝐷of 𝐴which factors 𝑓.
— 4. Every morphism 𝑓: 𝐴→𝐵admits an image (resp. a coimage) which is identified
with ker(coker 𝑓) (resp. with coker(ker 𝑓)).
— 5. (Factorization of morphisms) Every morphism 𝐴→𝐵in A factors uniquely up to
isomorphism into 𝐴→𝐼→𝐵, where 𝐴→𝐼is an epimorphism, and 𝐼→𝐵is a monomorphism.
Moreover 𝐴→𝐼is the coimage of 𝐴→𝐵, and 𝐼→𝐵is its image.
The existence of kernel and image for all morphisms in A allows us to define the notion of
an exact sequence of morphisms in A. We assume the reader is sufficiently familiar with this
notion so as not to have to redefine it.
— 6. For any pair (𝐴, 𝐵) of objects of A, the set HomA(𝐴, 𝐵) is equipped with an abelian
group structure, whose neutral element is the distinguished element 0 whose existence is

addition on the HomA(𝐴, 𝐵).
— 7. For any pair (𝐴, 𝐵) of objects of A, the product 𝐴× 𝐵and the sum 𝐴⊕𝐵whose

The existence of limits is of course an important property of categories. This leads us to
consider the following axioms:
AB4. Every family of objects admits a product in A.
AB4∗. Every family of objects admits a direct sum in A.
An abelian category satisfying AB4. is said to be complete, while an abelian category
satisfying AB4∗. is said to be cocomplete.
AB5. A is cocomplete, and for any increasing directed system 𝑀𝑖of subobjects of 𝑀, for any
subobject 𝑁of 𝑀, we have

A Grothendieck category is a cocomplete abelian category satisfying AB5.
Let us continue our list of properties of abelian categories.
— 10. Suppose that A is complete (resp. cocomplete). Then any diagram 𝐷: I →A,
admits a limit (resp. colimit). In particular, A admits inverse (resp. direct) limits.

A.VI. Abelian categories
257
A.VI.3
Jordan-Hölder series and composition factors
Let A be an abelian category. A non-zero object 𝑀of A is said to be simple if every subobject
of 𝑀is either the zero object 0, or 𝑀. We denote by Irr(A) the set of equivalence classes of
simple objects of A. Suppose that we have an increasing sequence of subobjects of 𝑀, all
distinct
0 = 𝐵0 ⊂𝐵1 ⊂· · · ⊂𝐵𝑛= 𝑀.
Such a sequence is called a composition series, or Jordan-Hölder series if the successive
quotients 𝐵𝑖/𝐵𝑖−1, 𝑖= 1, . . . 𝑛are simple. The integer 𝑛is called the length of this composition
series and the quotients 𝐵𝑖/𝐵𝑖−1 are called composition factors of 𝑀. The Jordan-Hölder
theorem then asserts that if the object 𝑀admits a composition series, then all composition
series are of the same length and their factors are isomorphic, up to permutation. An object
admitting a composition series is said to be of finite length, its length being that of one of its
composition series. The Jordan-Hölder theorem is proved from the Noether isomorphisms
A.VI.2, 9.
To obtain the results on the series and composition factors of an object generalizing those
of the category of 𝐴-modules, we must make some additional hypotheses on A. We therefore
assume that A is a Grothendieck category. It therefore satisfies AB4∗and AB5. We assume
moreover:
AB6 1: every object of A is a sum of finitely generated subobjects.
Examples. Note that the categories 𝐴−mod (respectively M(𝐴)), 𝐴a ring (respectively 𝐴a
unital ring) satisfy all these axioms.
The first result we will establish uses Zorn’s lemma. Zorn’s lemma can be considered as an
axiom of set theory. It is equivalent to the axiom of choice. We say that an ordered set 𝑆is
inductively ordered if any non-empty totally ordered subset of 𝑆admits an upper bound. We
refer the reader to [36] or [25] for the definitions of these terms. Zorn’s lemma asserts that a
non-empty inductively ordered set admits a maximal element.
Proposition. (𝑖) Let 𝑀be a non-zero finitely generated object of A. Then 𝑀admits a simple
quotient.
(𝑖𝑖) If 𝑀is not finitely generated, there exist subobjects 𝑁1 ⊂𝑁2 of 𝑀such that the
quotient 𝑁2/𝑁1 is simple.
Proof. It is clear that (𝑖𝑖) follows from (𝑖) by taking for 𝑁2 a finitely generated subobject in
𝑀, whose existence is ensured by AB6. To prove (𝑖), let us introduce the (ordered) set 𝑆of
proper subobjects of 𝑀. To be able to apply Zorn’s lemma, we now show that 𝑆is inductive,
i.e., that if (𝑁𝑖)𝑖is a chain in 𝑆(a totally ordered subset) then 𝑁:= Ð
𝑖𝑁𝑖is in 𝑆. We must
show that 𝑁is a proper submodule. Suppose the contrary, i.e., 𝑀= Ð
𝑖𝑁𝑖. Since 𝑀is finitely
generated, there exists a certain index 𝑖0 such that 𝑀= 𝑁𝑖0, which is absurd since 𝑁𝑖0 is a
proper submodule. The assertion is therefore proved. By Zorn’s lemma, 𝑆admits a maximal
element 𝑀0, and by maximality, the quotient 𝑀/𝑀0 is simple.
□
Let JH(𝑀) denote the set of simple subquotients of an object 𝑀. We then have:
Lemma. (𝑖) If 𝑀′ ⊂𝑀is a subobject, then
JH(𝑀) = JH(𝑀′) ∪JH(𝑀/𝑀′).

Corollary. A subobject of a semi-simple object is semi-simple.
Proof. Suppose that 𝐹is a subobject of 𝐸with 𝐸semi-simple, and let 𝐹′ be a subobject of 𝐹.
It is also a subobject of 𝐸, and thus there exists a subobject 𝐺of 𝐸such that 𝐸= 𝐹′ ⊕𝐺. Set
𝐺′ = 𝐺∩𝐹. We then have 𝐹= 𝐹′ ⊕𝐺′.
□
A.VIII
Notable functors
By notable functors, we mean functors preserving properties of the objects or morphisms to
which they apply. For example, we defined above faithful functors and full functors. We give
other examples.
A.VIII.1
Exact functors
Let 𝐹: A →B be a functor between two abelian categories. We say that 𝐹is additive if it
respects the group structure of the Hom, i.e., if for any pair (𝑋, 𝑌) of objects of A,
𝐹: HomA(𝑋, 𝑌) →HomB(𝐹(𝑋), 𝐹(𝑌))
is a morphism of abelian groups. Functors between abelian categories will implicitly always
be assumed to be additive. In what follows, the categories are abelian and the functors are
additive.

The first of these functors is covariant, the second contravariant. They are both left exact.
An object 𝑋of A is said to be projective if HomA(𝑋, •) is exact, injective if HomA(•, 𝑋) is
exact. It is a generator of A (resp. a cogenerator ) if HomA(𝑋, •) (resp. HomA(•, 𝑋)) is
faithful. A progenerator is a projective generator. A simple criterion is that a projective
object 𝑋is a progenerator in A if and only if for any non-zero object 𝑌in A, HomA(𝑋, 𝑌) is
non-trivial. This condition is sufficient by virtue of Lemma A.VIII.1.
Let A be a cocomplete abelian category. A projective object 𝑃in A is said to be small if the
functor HomA(𝑃, •) preserves direct sums. There exist several equivalent characterizations of
this notion. In particular, 𝑃is a small projective, if for any direct sum 𝑀= É
𝑖∈𝐼𝑀𝑖, and for any
morphism 𝜙: 𝑃→𝑀, there exists a finite set 𝐽⊂𝐼such that 𝜙factors into 𝑃→É
𝑗∈𝐽𝑀𝑖→𝑀.
In a Grothendieck category, an object is a small projective if and only if it is finitely generated
projective (cf. [37], 4.11, Lemma 1). The following result gives us a criterion for a cocomplete
abelian category to be equivalent to the category of right modules over a certain ring R (cf.
[37], 4.11, Theorem 1).
Theorem. Let A be an abelian category, cocomplete, and admitting a small progenerator
𝑃. Then A is naturally equivalent to the category of unital right modules over the ring
R𝑃= EndA(𝑃), the equivalence being given by the functor HomA(𝑃, •).

A.X
Center of a category
Let 𝒞be a category. The center 𝔷(𝒞) of 𝒞is the set of endomorphisms (i.e., natural transfor-
mations to itself) of the identity functor. In other words, an element 𝑧of the center of 𝒞is
the data, for any object 𝑋of 𝒞, of a morphism 𝑧𝑋: 𝑋→𝑋, so that given two objects 𝑋and 𝑌
of 𝒞, and a morphism 𝑓: 𝑋→𝑌, we have 𝑓◦𝑧𝑋= 𝑧𝑌◦𝑓.
Example. If 𝒞= M(𝐴), the category of unital 𝐴-modules over the unital ring 𝐴, then 𝔷(𝒞) is
naturally identified with the center of the ring 𝐴.

B.I
Amitsur’s theorem
The main result of this section is due to Amitsur. It can be viewed as a non-commutative
version of Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz for algebras over the field C.
Definition. Let 𝐴be a non zero C-algebra with identity 1𝐴. The spectrum of an element
𝑎of 𝐴is the set
Spec(𝑎) = {𝜆∈C | 𝑎−𝜆1𝐴is not invertible }
We identify the subalgebra C1𝐴of 𝐴with C.
Theorem. Let 𝐴be a non zero C-algebra with identity 1𝐴. Suppose that 𝐴is of at most
countable dimension over C. Then
𝑎) if 𝐴is a division algebra, 𝐴= C,
𝑏) for all 𝑎∈𝐴, Spec(𝑎) is non-empty. Moreover Spec(𝑎) = {0} if and only if 𝑎is
nilpotent.
Proof. We will show that 𝑏) implies 𝑎). Suppose there exists 𝑎∈𝐴, 𝑎∉C. Then for all 𝜆∈C,
𝑎−𝜆1𝐴is non-zero, hence invertible if we assume that 𝐴is a division algebra. This gives us
Spec(𝑎) = ∅, which contradicts 𝑏).
Let us now prove 𝑏). Suppose Spec(𝑎) = ∅, i.e., that for all 𝜆∈C, 𝑎−𝜆1𝐴is invertible. The
uncountable family
{(𝑎−𝜆1𝐴)−1 |𝜆∈C}
of elements of 𝐴is therefore linearly dependent, since the dimension of 𝐴is at most countable.
This shows that there exist a finite number of elements 𝜇𝑖∈C, which we can assume to be
non-zero, and the same number of elements 𝜆𝑖in C such that

Let 𝐴be a C-algebra (which we do not necessarily assume to be unital). We will use the result
of the previous section to show the
Theorem. Let 𝑀be a simple 𝐴-module and a C-vector space. We then have
𝑎) End𝐴(𝑀) is a division algebra.
𝑏) If the dimension of 𝑀over C is at most countable, we have

To do this, it suffices that the dimension of 𝐴be at most countable.
Proof. Note first that by replacing 𝐴by e𝐴= 𝐴⊕C1𝐴where as in Section I.2.1 we add an
identity to 𝐴, we can assume that 𝐴is a unital algebra and 𝑀a unital 𝐴-module.
Let 0 ≠𝑓∈End𝐴(𝑀). Since ker 𝑓and Im 𝑓are submodules of 𝑀, and 𝑀is simple, we have
ker 𝑓= 0 and Im 𝑓= 𝑀. This shows that 𝑓is invertible.
Let us now show that if the dimension of 𝐴is at most countable, the same is true for that
of 𝑀. Consider 𝑚∈𝑀such that 𝐴· 𝑚≠0 and

Since 𝑀is simple, 𝐴· 𝑚= 𝑀and thus 𝑀is of at most countable dimension over C.
Similarly, let us prove 𝑏): consider
End𝐴(𝑀) →𝑀,
𝑓↦→𝑓(𝑚).
Since 𝑓(𝑎· 𝑚) = 𝑎· 𝑓(𝑚) and 𝐴· 𝑚= 𝑀, we see that 𝑓is determined by 𝑓(𝑚), and thus that the
morphism above is injective. We deduce that the dimension of End𝐴(𝑀) is at most countable.
Amitsur’s theorem then shows that End𝐴(𝑀) ≃C.

B.III
Separation lemma
We begin with a well-known characterization of the Jacobson radical.


## Page 271


B.III. Separation lemma
265
Lemma. Let 𝐴be a unital ring and let 𝐽(𝐴) denote its Jacobson radical, defined as the
intersection of all maximal left ideals of 𝐴. An element 𝑟∈𝐴belongs to 𝐽(𝐴) if and only if
for all 𝑥∈𝐴, the element 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟is left invertible in 𝐴. Furthermore, if this condition holds,
the element 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟is in fact two-sided invertible in 𝐴.
Proof. Assume first that 𝑟∈𝐽(𝐴). Let 𝑥∈𝐴and suppose for the sake of contradiction that
1𝐴−𝑥𝑟is not left invertible. Then the left ideal 𝐴(1𝐴−𝑥𝑟) is proper, as it does not contain 1𝐴.
Consequently, it is contained in some maximal left ideal 𝔪. Since 𝑟∈𝐽(𝐴), we have 𝑟∈𝔪,
which implies 𝑥𝑟∈𝔪because 𝔪is a left ideal. By construction, 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟∈𝔪. Adding these
two elements yields 1𝐴= (1𝐴−𝑥𝑟) + 𝑥𝑟∈𝔪, which contradicts the fact that 𝔪is a proper
ideal. Thus, 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟must be left invertible.
Conversely, assume that for all 𝑥∈𝐴, the element 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟is left invertible. Suppose for
the sake of contradiction that 𝑟∉𝐽(𝐴). Then there exists a maximal left ideal 𝔪such that
𝑟∉𝔪. Because 𝔪is maximal, the left ideal 𝔪+ 𝐴𝑟must be the entire ring 𝐴. Therefore, there
exist 𝑚∈𝔪and 𝑥∈𝐴such that 𝑚+ 𝑥𝑟= 1𝐴. This implies 𝑚= 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟. By our hypothesis,
1𝐴−𝑥𝑟is left invertible, meaning 𝑚has a left inverse. This implies 𝐴𝑚= 𝐴, so 1𝐴∈𝔪, which
contradicts that 𝔪is a proper ideal. Hence, 𝑟∈𝐽(𝐴).
Finally, we show that the left inverse of 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟is a two-sided inverse. Let 𝑢= 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟
and let 𝑣∈𝐴be its left inverse, so 𝑣𝑢= 1𝐴. We can write 𝑣= 1𝐴−(−𝑣)𝑥𝑟. Since 𝑟∈𝐽(𝐴)
and 𝐽(𝐴) is a two-sided ideal, the element (−𝑣)𝑥𝑟is in 𝐽(𝐴). By the first part of the proof,
𝑣= 1𝐴−(−𝑣)𝑥𝑟must also have a left inverse, say 𝑤∈𝐴, such that 𝑤𝑣= 1𝐴. We then have
𝑤= 𝑤(𝑣𝑢) = (𝑤𝑣)𝑢= 1𝐴𝑢= 𝑢. Therefore, 𝑢is the left inverse of 𝑣, meaning 𝑢𝑣= 1𝐴. Thus, 𝑣
is a two-sided inverse, and 1𝐴−𝑥𝑟is invertible in 𝐴.
□
We will now show the following separation result:
Proposition. Let 𝐴be an algebra over C of countable dimension. Let 𝑎be a non-nilpotent
element of 𝐴. Then there exists a simple left module 𝑀such that 𝑎· 𝑀≠0.
Proof. By replacing 𝐴with e𝐴= 𝐴⊕C1𝐴if necessary, we may assume that 𝐴is a unital algebra.
We first show that 𝑎∉𝐽(𝐴), where 𝐽(𝐴) denotes the Jacobson radical, as in the lemma
above. Since 𝑎is not nilpotent, Theorem B.I, part 𝑏), gives Spec(𝑎) ≠{0}. Combined with
the non-emptiness of Spec(𝑎), this yields some 𝜆∈Spec(𝑎) with 𝜆≠0, i.e., 𝑎−𝜆1𝐴is not
invertible.
Set 𝜇= 𝜆−1 ∈C×. Then
1𝐴−𝜇𝑎= −𝜇(𝑎−𝜆1𝐴),
a nonzero scalar multiple of a non-invertible element, hence itself not invertible. Taking
𝑥= 𝜇1𝐴in the characterization above shows 𝑎∉𝐽(𝐴).
Since 𝑎∉𝐽(𝐴) = Ñ
𝔪𝔪, there exists a maximal left ideal 𝔪of 𝐴with 𝑎∉𝔪. Set 𝑀= 𝐴/𝔪:
this is a simple left 𝐴-module. Finally, 𝑎·𝑀= 0 would mean 𝑎·1𝐴∈𝔪, i.e., 𝑎∈𝔪, contradicting
the choice of 𝔪. Hence 𝑎· 𝑀≠0.

C.I

We prove the following result on commutative subalgebras of End(𝑉):
Lemma. Let 𝑉be a vector space over C of dimension 𝑘and let 𝑅be a commutative subalgebra
of End(𝑉) generated by 𝑙elements 𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝑙and the identity. Then dim 𝑅≤𝑓𝑙(𝑘) = 𝑘2−21−𝑙.

characteristic subspaces of the endomorphism 𝑎1. These are subspaces stable under the action
of 𝑅. We decompose each of them into characteristic subspaces for the action of 𝑎2. We
obtain a finer decomposition into subspaces stable under the action of 𝑅. Let us reiterate the
operation up to 𝑎𝑙. We thus decompose 𝑉into a direct sum of subspaces stable under the
action of 𝑅, say 𝑚subspaces of dimension 𝑘1, . . . , 𝑘𝑚, where each 𝑎𝑖is the sum of a scalar
operator and a nilpotent operator. Since 2 −21−𝑙≥1, 𝑓𝑙is convex 𝑓𝑙(0) = 0, we have
𝑓𝑙(𝑘) ≥𝑓𝑙(𝑘1) + · · · + 𝑓𝑙(𝑘𝑚)
and it suffices to prove the lemma for each subspace. We can therefore assume that the 𝑎𝑖are

by their nilpotent parts, and thus ultimately assume them to be nilpotent.
Let 𝜙𝑙(𝑘) denote the largest possible dimension of a commutative subalgebra 𝑅of End(𝑉)

Example 5) is a finitely generated 𝐵-module. Then 𝑎is eventually stable. Let 𝑛∈N∗such
that 𝑎𝑛is stable, and let 𝐾= ker 𝑎𝑛, 𝐼= Im 𝑎𝑛. Then 𝐿= 𝐾⊕𝐼and the localization (𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎) is
isomorphic to (𝐼, 𝑎|𝐼).

(𝑏· 𝜙)(𝑣) = 𝑏· (𝜙(𝑣)) = 𝜙(𝑏· 𝑣),
(𝜙∈End𝐵(𝐿𝑎), 𝑏∈𝐵, 𝑣∈𝐿𝑎).

C.III. Representations of Z𝑑

Since 𝐿𝑎is a finitely generated 𝐵-module, it is easy to see that End𝐵(𝐿𝑎) is as well, and thus
we can find elements 𝑏𝑖∈𝐵, 𝑖= 1, . . . , 𝑚such that
˜𝑎−𝑚+ 𝑏1˜𝑎−𝑚+1 + · · · + 𝑏𝑚= 0
whence ˜𝑎−1 = −(𝑏1 + 𝑏2˜𝑎+ · · · + 𝑏𝑚˜𝑎𝑚−1), and thus ˜𝑎−1 ∈𝐵[˜𝑎]. We will now show that the
morphism 𝜄: 𝐿→𝐿𝑎(A.III.1, Example 5) is surjective. It is clear that ˜𝑎stabilizes 𝜄(𝐿), and since
˜𝑎−1 ∈𝐵[˜𝑎], ˜𝑎−1 stabilizes 𝜄(𝐿) and ˜𝑎|𝜄(𝐿) is therefore invertible. Consider the pair (𝜄(𝐿), ˜𝑎). It is
also manifestly a solution of the same universal problem as (𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎). We deduce the surjectivity
of 𝜄.
We now use the lemma and Corollary A.III.1, which show that (𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎) is isomorphic to
(𝐿′, 𝑎′), where 𝐿′ = 𝐿/𝐾, with 𝐾= ∪𝑛∈N ker 𝑎𝑛. Since 𝐿𝑎and 𝐿are finitely generated 𝐵-modules,
𝐾is as well. Consequently, there exists a non-zero integer 𝑛such that 𝐾= ker 𝑎𝑛. Set 𝐼= Im 𝑎𝑛.
We then have 𝐿= 𝐾⊕𝐼. Since 𝜄is surjective, it induces an isomorphism (𝐼, 𝑎𝑚) ≃(𝐿𝑎, ˜𝑎𝑚). Now
˜𝑎𝑚is invertible, and thus 𝑎|𝐼is invertible.
□
C.III

## Page 288


Representations of 𝑝-adic Reductive Groups
David Renard
This book presents a part of the (complex) representation theory of 𝑝-adic reductive groups.
Starting from a basis accessible to graduate students, it culminates with the theory of the
"Bernstein center" and the Langlands classification of irreducible smooth representations.
The book consists of seven chapters, Chapters VI and VII constituting the core of the book.
Chapter VI is devoted to the study of the category of smooth representations of a 𝑝-adic
reductive group, establishing among other things the Bernstein decomposition theorem and
the description of its center. Chapter VII deals with square-integrable and tempered represen-
tations, and the Langlands classification theorem is proved there.
The first four chapters are placed in a more general framework and tackle, in order, the study
of algebras with idempotents, totally disconnected locally compact spaces and groups, smooth
representations of the latter, and particular classes of representations (compact, unitary,
square-integrable). Chapter V is a review of the structure results of 𝑝-adic reductive groups.
Appendices give the elements of category theory and some results in algebra used in the text.
